phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093\nTable~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation} establishes that DR violations are pervasive (15--18\\% across datasets) and that constraint layers interact non-additively: grammar-only achieves 3\\% DR reduction on SciERC, semantic-only 24\\%, but their composition yields 94\\%---the \\emph{empirical enabling dependency}.\nOn DocRED (96 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$), grammar-only \\emph{increases} DR via \\textit{latent violation surfacing}: forcing OOV$\\to$valid relation mapping exposes violations previously masked by vocabulary errors (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\nCross-model validation (3 models $\\times$ 2 datasets; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model}) confirms DR drops of 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED, with R-F1 improving in 5/6 settings; post-hoc filtering achieves DR${=}$0\\% but at 15--74\\% rejection rates.\nDR-to-CF conversion represents a severity downgrade: DR is irrecoverable without re-extraction, while CF---individually valid triples with cross-triple inconsistency---is amenable to post-hoc resolution (TCR eliminates all CF in our experiments, with bounded DR regression; Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}).\nZero-shot evaluation thus establishes violation prevalence and constraint interaction; fine-tuning demonstrates practical elimination.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning (expanded)\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\n\\label{sec:composability}\n% exp_ids: bootstrap_ci_ft_composability.json, w5_lora_scierc_*, w8_*\nFine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\\% but leaves residual violations on tight-constraint relations (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}); adding S1 yields DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\% with no significant $\\Delta$R-F1 (Bonferroni-corrected; 95\\% CIs $\\subseteq [-0.69, {+}0.25]$\\,pp; removed triples are 97.6--100\\% false positives; Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream-impact}).\nUnder fine-tuning, grammar becomes a no-op ($\\Delta$DR$<$0.2\\,pp), making the semantic layer the sole DR defense (39--72\\% reduction; Appendix~\\ref{app:ft-ablation}); three relations with $\\kappa \\geq 0.36$ retain DR, all eliminated by S1 (Appendix~\\ref{app:lora-per-relation}).\nFallback frequency is model-dependent: Qwen triggers 0 fallbacks under LoRA + S1 on SciERC, while Llama triggers 5{,}882 (Table~\\ref{tab:ft-ablation}), reflecting weaker entity typing; despite this, DR reaches 0.09\\% on SciERC (0.21\\% on DocRED), confirming that even frequent fallback does not compromise enforcement.\nCF drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned), concentrated in \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X ambiguity (63.3\\% of zero-shot CF; Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types at 8B (R-F1 43--46; \\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  CF quantification (MF1 response)\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Cross-field violation analysis.}\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1 (ZS: CF=248, 167 docs), w5_lora_scierc_full_s1_eval (FT: CF=22, 20 docs)\nUnder zero-shot greedy S1, CF is the sole residual violation type (DR, RV, ET all eliminated): 248 cross-field violations arise across 167 documents, of which 63.3\\% involve \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X type ambiguity (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}), concentrated in \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$\\textsc{OtherScientificTerm} (34.7\\%) and \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$\\textsc{Material} (12.3\\%).\nA source breakdown (Appendix~\\ref{app:cascade-analysis}) shows 58.1\\% originate from hallucinated entities (a model capacity issue), while true cascade errors---where first-seen registration locks an incorrect type---account for only 7.3\\%; correct locks are 2.5$\\times$ more frequent, confirming first-seen registration as a net-positive mechanism.\nFine-tuning reduces CF to 22 cross-field violation instances across 20 documents (1.18\\%), with cascade errors completely eliminated (0/22).\nBeam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all remaining CF at 1.39$\\times$ latency (\\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrrr r@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Mode}\n  & \\textbf{DR\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{CF}\n  & \\textbf{R-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} & \\textbf{E-F1\\,$\\uparrow$}\n  & \\textbf{$\\Delta$DR} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.95 & 0    & 43.86 & 67.05 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    & \\textbf{0} & 1.18 & 43.77 & 67.05 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.76 & 0.12 & 40.86 & 82.15 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    & 0.01 & 0.41 & 40.56 & 81.43 & $\\downarrow$98.7\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{Llama\\\\3.1-8B}}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.11 & 0    & 45.45 & 67.76 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    &  0.09 & 0.46 & 45.47 & 67.76 & $\\downarrow$92\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.49 & 0.08 & 43.72 & 83.93 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    &  0.21 & 0.21 & 43.78 & 83.94 & $\\downarrow$57\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Fine-tuning composability: LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1 on SciERC (551 docs) and DocRED (998 dev docs).\nF1 (\\%). No $\\Delta$R-F1 is significant (Bonferroni-corrected; details in text); all DR reductions are ($p{<}0.001$).\nE-F1 includes parse-failure documents (Qwen PF: 7--8\\%; Llama PF: 2--3\\%).}\n\\label{tab:composability}\n\\end{table}\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (f) Downstream impact of DR elimination\n%  exp_ids: task_e_lucky_violation_analysis\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Downstream impact.}\n\\label{sec:downstream-impact}\nAmong constraint-removed triples, only 0.49\\% match gold labels (Appendix~\\ref{app:lucky-violations}); error analysis of 514 false negatives confirms only one (0.2\\%) was blocked by constraints, with dominant sources (relation confusion, entity type polysemy) schema-valid and beyond constraint reach (Appendix~\\ref{app:error-analysis}).\nAcross fine-tuned settings, 36 residual DR violations decompose into entity type errors (39\\%), schema coverage gaps (33\\%), and relation/direction errors (28\\%); the irreducible floor is $\\sim$3 violations (Appendix~\\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).\n",

S1/S2 = single/two-pass (\\S\\ref{sec:strategies}).\nGrammar = GCD ceiling~\\cite{geng2023gcd}.\n$\\dagger$vLLM \\texttt{guided\\_json}~\\cite{kwon2023vllm} (\\S\\ref{sec:constrained-baselines}).\nDeterministic (greedy, $T{=}$0); phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093\nTable~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation} establishes that DR violations are pervasive (15--18\\% across datasets) and that constraint layers interact non-additively: grammar-only achieves 3\\% DR reduction on SciERC, semantic-only 24\\%, but their composition yields 94\\%---the \\emph{empirical enabling dependency}.\nOn DocRED (96 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$), grammar-only \\emph{increases} DR via \\textit{latent violation surfacing}: forcing OOV$\\to$valid relation mapping exposes violations previously masked by vocabulary errors (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\nCross-model validation (3 models $\\times$ 2 datasets; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model}) confirms DR drops of 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED, with R-F1 improving in 5/6 settings; post-hoc filtering achieves DR${=}$0\\% but at 15--74\\% rejection rates.\nDR-to-CF conversion represents a severity downgrade: DR is irrecoverable without re-extraction, while CF---individually valid triples with cross-triple inconsistency---is amenable to post-hoc resolution (TCR eliminates all CF in our experiments, with bounded DR regression; Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}).\nZero-shot evaluation thus establishes violation prevalence and constraint interaction; fine-tuning demonstrates practical elimination.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning (expanded)\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\n\\label{sec:composability}\n% exp_ids: bootstrap_ci_ft_composability.json, w5_lora_scierc_*, w8_*\nFine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\\% but leaves residual violations on tight-constraint relations (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}); adding S1 yields DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\% with no significant $\\Delta$R-F1 (Bonferroni-corrected; 95\\% CIs $\\subseteq [-0.69, {+}0.25]$\\,pp; removed triples are 97.6--100\\% false positives; Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream-impact}).\nUnder fine-tuning, grammar becomes a no-op ($\\Delta$DR$<$0.2\\,pp), making the semantic layer the sole DR defense (41--75\\% on SciERC/DocRED; Appendix~\\ref{app:ft-ablation}); three relations with $\\kappa \\geq 0.36$ retain DR, all eliminated by S1 (Appendix~\\ref{app:lora-per-relation}).\nFallback frequency is model-dependent: Qwen triggers 0 fallbacks under LoRA + S1 on SciERC, while Llama triggers 5{,}882 (Table~\\ref{tab:ft-ablation}), reflecting weaker entity typing; despite this, DR reaches 0.09\\% on SciERC (0.21\\% on DocRED), confirming that even frequent fallback does not compromise enforcement.\nCF drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned), concentrated in \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X ambiguity (63.3\\% of zero-shot CF; Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types at 8B (R-F1 43--46; \\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  CF quantification (MF1 response)\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Cross-field violation analysis.}\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1 (ZS: CF=248, 167 docs), w5_lora_scierc_full_s1_eval (FT: CF=22, 20 docs)\nUnder zero-shot greedy S1, CF is the sole residual violation type (DR, RV, ET all eliminated): 248 cross-field violations arise across 167 documents, of which 63.3\\% involve \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X type ambiguity (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}), concentrated in \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$\\textsc{OtherScientificTerm} (34.7\\%) and \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$\\textsc{Material} (12.3\\%).\nA source breakdown (Appendix~\\ref{app:cascade-analysis}) shows 58.1\\% originate from hallucinated entities (a model capacity issue), while true cascade errors---where first-seen registration locks an incorrect type---account for only 7.3\\%; correct locks are 2.5$\\times$ more frequent, confirming first-seen registration as a net-positive mechanism.\nFine-tuning reduces CF to 22 cross-field violation instances across 20 documents (1.18\\%), with cascade errors completely eliminated (0/22).\nBeam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all remaining CF at 1.39$\\times$ latency (\\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrrr r@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Mode}\n  & \\textbf{DR\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{CF}\n  & \\textbf{R-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} & \\textbf{E-F1\\,$\\uparrow$}\n  & \\textbf{$\\Delta$DR} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.95 & 0    & 43.86 & 67.05 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    & \\textbf{0} & 1.18 & 43.77 & 67.05 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.76 & 0.12 & 40.86 & 82.15 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    & 0.01 & 0.41 & 40.56 & 81.43 & $\\downarrow$98.7\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{Llama\\\\3.1-8B}}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.11 & 0    & 45.45 & 67.76 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    &  0.09 & 0.46 & 45.47 & 67.76 & $\\downarrow$92\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.49 & 0.08 & 43.72 & 83.93 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    &  0.21 & 0.21 & 43.78 & 83.94 & $\\downarrow$57\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Fine-tuning composability: LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1 on SciERC (551 docs) and DocRED (998 dev docs).\nF1 (\\%). No $\\Delta$R-F1 is significant (Bonferroni-corrected; details in text); all DR reductions are ($p{<}0.001$).\nE-F1 includes parse-failure documents (Qwen PF: 7--8\\%; Llama PF: 2--3\\%).}\n\\label{tab:composability}\n\\end{table}\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (f) Downstream impact of DR elimination\n%  exp_ids: task_e_lucky_violation_analysis\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Downstream impact.}\n\\label{sec:downstream-impact}\nAmong constraint-removed triples, only 0.49\\% match gold labels (Appendix~\\ref{app:lucky-violations}); error analysis of 514 false negatives confirms only one (0.2\\%) was blocked by constraints, with dominant sources (relation confusion, entity type polysemy) schema-valid and beyond constraint reach (Appendix~\\ref{app:error-analysis}).\nAcross fine-tuned settings, 36 residual DR violations decompose into entity type errors (39\\%), schema coverage gaps (33\\%), and relation/direction errors (28\\%); the irreducible floor is $\\sim$3 violations (Appendix~\\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).\n",

_source line 8415 · 2026-05-23T11:53:30.344Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01TFSuzRYaarLrf2sMexvqHT`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Large language models for information extraction generate structurally valid but ontologically invalid triples---where entity types violate the ontology's domain/range constraints.
3	These violations persist across the capability spectrum and cannot be addressed by grammar-constrained decoding (which lacks cross-field type awareness) or post-hoc filtering (which discards valid predictions).
4	
5	We present \textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that compiles information extraction ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints.
6	A decidability analysis shows that domain/range constraints exceed context-free expressiveness, yielding a two-layer architecture: a grammar layer enforces structural validity and vocabulary membership; a semantic layer enforces type constraints at runtime through stateful masking over a dynamic entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.
7	
8	% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval
9	Evaluated across three 8B-class models and two relation extraction datasets (up to 96 relations), OntoCoDec composed with LoRA fine-tuning reduces domain/range violations to near-zero ($\leq$\,0.21\%) at F1 40--46\%, but introduces cross-field type inconsistencies ($\leq$1.4\%) as the dominant residual---an architectural consequence of per-triple independent enforcement; net total violations decrease ${\sim}$31\% in zero-shot and ${>}$90\% under fine-tuning.
10	Beam search ($k{=}2$) reduces CF to 0.11\% at 1.18$\times$ latency; $k{=}4$ eliminates all violation types at 8B at 1.39$\times$.
11	In zero-shot settings, cross-field rates are higher (up to 12\%) but decrease with model calibration.
12	Zero-shot domain/range reductions span 76--99\% on SciERC and 42--90\% on DocRED; violation rates follow an observed pattern consistent with schema restrictiveness across our three evaluation schemas ($n{=}3$; insufficient for statistical inference), with a scale-dependent relationship between the two layers.
13	\end{abstract}
14	
```

```json
    "newString": "a semantic layer enforces type constraints at runtime through stateful masking over a dynamic entity registry.",
    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nLarge language models for information extraction generate structurally valid but ontologically invalid triples---where entity types violate the ontology's domain/range constraints.\nThese violations persist across the capability spectrum and cannot be addressed by grammar-constrained decoding (which lacks cross-field type awareness) or post-hoc filtering (which discards valid predictions).\n\nWe present \\textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that compiles information extraction ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints.\nA decidability analysis shows that domain/range constraints exceed context-free expressiveness, yielding a two-layer architecture: a grammar layer enforces structural validity and vocabulary membership; a semantic layer enforces type constraints at runtime through stateful masking over a dynamic entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.\n\n% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\nEvaluated across three 8B-class models and two relation extraction datasets (up to 96 relations), OntoCoDec composed with LoRA fine-tuning reduces domain/range violations to near-zero ($\\leq$\\,0.21\\%) at F1 40--46\\%, but introduces cross-field type inconsistencies ($\\leq$1.4\\%) as the dominant residual---an architectural consequence of per-triple independent enforcement; net total violations decrease ${\\sim}$31\\% in zero-shot and ${>}$90\\% under fine-tuning.\nBeam search ($k{=}2$) reduces CF to 0.11\\% at 1.18$\\times$ latency; $k{=}4$ eliminates all violation types at 8B at 1.39$\\times$.\nIn zero-shot settings, cross-field rates are higher (up to 12\\%) but decrease with model calibration.\nZero-shot domain/range reductions span 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED; violation rates follow an observed pattern consistent with schema restrictiveness across our three evaluation schemas ($n{=}3$; insufficient for statistical inference), with a scale-dependent relationship between the two layers.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
```

```json
    "newString": "($\\leq$1.4\\%) from per-triple enforcement.",
    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nLarge language models for information extraction generate structurally valid but ontologically invalid triples---where entity types violate the ontology's domain/range constraints.\nThese violations persist across the capability spectrum and cannot be addressed by grammar-constrained decoding (which lacks cross-field type awareness) or post-hoc filtering (which discards valid predictions).\n\nWe present \\textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that compiles information extraction ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints.\nA decidability analysis shows that domain/range constraints exceed context-free expressiveness, yielding a two-layer architecture: a grammar layer enforces structural validity and vocabulary membership; a semantic layer enforces type constraints at runtime through stateful masking over a dynamic entity registry.\n\n% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\nEvaluated across three 8B-class models and two relation extraction datasets (up to 96 relations), OntoCoDec composed with LoRA fine-tuning reduces domain/range violations to near-zero ($\\leq$\\,0.21\\%) at F1 40--46\\%, but introduces cross-field type inconsistencies ($\\leq$1.4\\%) as the dominant residual---an architectural consequence of per-triple independent enforcement; net total violations decrease ${\\sim}$31\\% in zero-shot and ${>}$90\\% under fine-tuning.\nBeam search ($k{=}2$) reduces CF to 0.11\\% at 1.18$\\times$ latency; $k{=}4$ eliminates all violation types at 8B at 1.39$\\times$.\nIn zero-shot settings, cross-field rates are higher (up to 12\\%) but decrease with model calibration.\nZero-shot domain/range reductions span 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED; violation rates follow an observed pattern consistent with schema restrictiveness across our three evaluation schemas ($n{=}3$; insufficient for statistical inference), with a scale-dependent relationship between the two layers.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
```

[Director 指令] [Patch] 提交前最后一轮 framing 优化——针对反复出现的审稿弱点做系统性修复，目标从 3.5 推向 4.0。

投稿目标：EMNLP 2026 ARR，DDL 5月25日。引用不需要验证。

## 修改清单（按优先级排序）

### P0: Contribution 重排序（R16-W5，跨 10+ 轮复发）

审稿人一致认为 Proposition 1 是 "formalizing known intuition"，工程可行性分析（DFA 6^17-6^19 states）比 pumping lemma 本身更有价值。论文重心应从理论转向实证+工程。

**具体修改：**
1. **§1 Contribution 列表重排序**：当前 C1=理论（decidability）, C2=实证（ablation）, C3=70B。改为：
   - C1 = 实证贡献（systematic ablation across 3 models × 2 datasets，DR≤0.21%，near-zero F1 cost）— 这是论文最强的贡献
   - C2 = 方法贡献（two-layer architecture grounded in decidability analysis，DFA bounds as engineering motivation，Proposition 1 作为 formal backing）
   - C3 = 保持不变（70B preliminary exploration）
2. **§3 压缩 Proposition 1 展示权重**：当前 Proposition 1 是 bolded numbered theorem（line 25）。改为：将重心转到 engineering applicability（DFA state spaces 6^17-6^19 使 naive CFG 不可行），Proposition 1 作为 formal justification 而非 headline。具体：Proposition 前面的 engineering motivation 段落扩展为主论述，Proposition 本身可缩短或降格为 "Formally, ..." 的 inline 引用。不要删除 Proposition 1 本身——只是降低展示权重。
3. **§7 opening sentence** 也要对应调整——不要以 Proposition 1 开头，而是以实证发现开头。

### P1: §7 Discussion 扩展（R25-W1 + R25-W2 + R24-SF5）

§7 当前 ~20 行。需扩展到 ~30 行。具体添加：

1. **Scale-dependent interaction 因果假设**（R25-W2）：在 §7 的 scale 讨论处添加 1-2 句机制解释。现有数据支持的假设：70B models assign higher probability to ontology-valid tokens (better vocabulary calibration)，导致 semantic-only 在 70B 达到 84% DR reduction vs 8B 的 24%。可引用 Appendix causal tracing 数据。当前 §7 L5 有一句 "likely reflecting improved vocabulary calibration"——在此基础上补充 fallback 频率差异的具体数据（如有的话，从 appendix 引用）。

2. **S1 vs S2 deployment guidance**（R25-W3）：当前 §7 L15 已有 S2 分析和 CRANE reference。确认这段是否充分——需要包含：S1 推荐作为默认，S2 在 70B 下 −8pp，CRANE 理论一致性。如果已经充分就不用改。

### P2: DocRED dev set 说明提升（R25-M3）

DocRED 使用 dev set (test annotations unavailable) 当前仅在 footnote 中。在 §5 Setup/Experimental Setup 段的正文中显著说明。1 句即可。

### P3: 验证以下已完成项

以下项目根据我的检查应该已经完成，请逐一确认并报告：
- R23-W4: WikiEvents 已从 Contribution 2 删除（C2 当前只覆盖 3 models × 2 RE datasets）
- R25-N2: GoLLIE 附录已改为定性讨论（无直接对比表）
- R16-W1: κ-DR §4 已压缩至 ~3 行（无 p 值）
- R23-W5: C3 使用 "Preliminary single-family scale exploration"
- R25-M2: CRANE 已在 §7 主文引用
- R25-M5: Schema evolution caveat 已在 Limitations
- R23-W12: Cascade error 已量化（7.3% true cascade, 58.1% hallucinated）

### 约束
- EMNLP 主文 8 页限制。如需空间，优先压缩 §3（降低 Proposition 1 展示权重时自然释放空间）
- Abstract 必须保持 ≤ 200 词（当前 189 词）
- 不要改动 Table 1 和 Table 2 的任何数据
- 编译通过后报告

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Vg2etAe4mm9nzXjJykDaFk`</summary>

```text
1	% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)
2	
3	\section{Introduction}
4	\label{sec:introduction}
5	
6	Large language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity necessarily carries a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.
7	We call such outputs \emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.
8	Violations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\% of triples on SciERC~\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\% on DocRED~\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 0.5--2\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates follow an observed pattern consistent with schema restrictiveness (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.
9	At competitive quality (R-F1 40--46\%), even 1--2\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption, making reliable constraint enforcement necessary.
10	
11	Existing approaches leave a fundamental gap.
12	Schema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \emph{increase} by 5.8\,pp).
13	Grammar-constrained decoding~\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} ensures syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.
14	Post-hoc filtering discards up to 74\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).
15	Training-based approaches~\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} internalize schema knowledge but provide only soft guidance without runtime enforcement.
16	
17	We propose \textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.
18	A decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\S\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \emph{grammar layer} compiles ontology constraints into context-free grammars (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \emph{semantic layer} interprets type constraints at runtime via dynamic logit masking over an entity registry, with a scale-dependent layer interaction: at 8B, the semantic layer achieves only 24\% independent DR reduction (near-complete when combined), while at 70B it reaches 84\% independently.
19	OntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and is applicable to autoregressive LMs with logits access; we validate on three English datasets (up to 96 relation types), three 8B-class models across three tokenizer families, with 70B-class validation (\S\ref{sec:70b-results}).
20	LoRA + greedy S1 reduces DR to near-zero: DR\,$\leq$\,0.21\% at R-F1 40--46\% ($\pm$0.3\,pp, $+$10\% latency); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types at 8B on both RE datasets under fine-tuning (1.39$\times$ latency).
21	DR enforcement converts ontologically invalid violations to individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF\,$\leq$\,1.4\% fine-tuned); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\% on SciERC and 42--90\% on DocRED, with total violations decreasing $\sim$31\% (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).
22	
23	OntoCoDec is a constraint enforcement method, orthogonal and complementary to training-based schema-aware approaches such as GoLLIE~\cite{sainz2024gollie} and KnowCoder~\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}; it can be composed with any generative IE system to improve schema compliance without retraining.
24	
25	Our contributions:
26	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]
27	    \item A two-layer constrained decoding framework grounded in the observation that domain/range type constraints require stateful enforcement beyond context-free grammars---grammar-only approaches face state spaces of $6^{17}$--$6^{19}$ for typical IE schemas (Proposition~1; \S\ref{sec:decidability}). Constraints compile automatically from IE ontologies, validated on schemas with up to 96 relation types.
28	    \item Systematic ablation across 3 model families (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B) and 2 datasets (SciERC, DocRED) under both zero-shot and LoRA fine-tuned settings, demonstrating DR reduction to $\leq$0.21\% with R-F1 within $\pm$0.3\,pp; DR enforcement introduces CF as a correctable side effect (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}).
29	    \item Preliminary single-family scale exploration on Llama-3.1-70B (AWQ INT4), revealing a scale-dependent layer interaction: the semantic layer alone achieves 84\% DR reduction at 70B vs.\ 24\% at 8B (\S\ref{sec:70b-results}). At 8B, beam $k{=}2$ is Pareto-optimal (R-F1 44.60, CF 0.11\%, 1.18$\times$ greedy S1 latency); $k{=}4$ achieves DR${=}$0\% with near-zero CF at 1.39$\times$.
30	\end{enumerate}
31	
```

```text
1	% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion
2	
3	OntoCoDec compiles IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints, validated across 3 datasets, 3+ models, and 2 tasks.
4	Because IE type constraints exceed context-free expressiveness (Proposition~1), the semantic layer bridges the gap grammar-only enforcement cannot address (3\% vs.\ 94\% DR reduction at 8B), using constructive forward masking over a dynamic entity registry.
5	This scale-dependent interaction (\S\ref{sec:70b-results})---likely reflecting improved vocabulary calibration at larger scale, where 70B models assign higher probability to ontology-valid tokens (Appendix~\ref{app:causal-tracing})---suggests grammar-constrained decoding is necessary but increasingly insufficient as models improve; constraint enforcement is preserved under INT4 quantization (4$\times$ compression; Appendix~\ref{app:quantization}).
6	
7	\paragraph{DR$\leftrightarrow$CF trade-off.}
8	Greedy S1 reduces DR to $\leq$\,0.21\% but introduces CF (zero-shot $+$9.82\,pp, fine-tuned $+$1.18\,pp)---an architectural consequence of per-triple independent enforcement.
9	Among 248 zero-shot CF violations, 58.1\% involve hallucinated entities (absent from gold) and only 7.3\% are true cascade errors (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}); fine-tuned CF drops to $\leq$1.4\%.
10	Beam $k{=}2$ reduces CF to 0.11\% at 1.18$\times$ latency (Pareto-optimal; recommended for zero-shot deployment); $k{=}4$ eliminates all violations at 8B (1 residual CF at 70B) at 1.39$\times$ (\S\ref{sec:beam-results}).
11	Net effect: total violations decrease ${\sim}$31\% in zero-shot and ${>}$90\% under fine-tuning; DR is irrecoverable while CF is amenable to post-hoc resolution (TCR; Appendix~\ref{app:tcr}).
12	
13	\paragraph{Complementarity and deployment.}
14	GoLLIE and KnowCoder internalize schema knowledge via training but lack runtime guarantees; LoRA + S1 shows the two are complementary (DR 1--2\%\,$\to$\,$\leq$0.21\%; neither alone achieves both high R-F1 and near-zero DR).
15	S2 (two-pass) exhibits scale-sensitive behavior: comparable to S1 at 8B but $-$8\,pp R-F1 at 70B, consistent with CRANE's~\cite{banerjee2025crane} finding that aggressive constraint enforcement can interfere with well-calibrated reasoning; we recommend S1 as the default configuration.
16	
17	\paragraph{Per-triple vs.\ global enforcement.}
18	Per-triple enforcement cannot coordinate cross-triple type assignments---the source of CF (Level~3; \S\ref{sec:decidability}); global entity-level type consensus exceeds online per-token masking.
19	Beam search and TCR approximate this; integrating consensus into decoding remains future work.
20	
```

```text
1	% Section 3 — Method (Draft)
2	% OntoCoDec: Type-Valid Information Extraction
3	% via Compiled Ontology Constraints
4	
5	\section{Method}
6	\label{sec:method}
7	
8	\input{fig1_framework}
9	
10	We present OntoCoDec, a framework that translates IE ontologies into token-level constraints enforced during autoregressive generation.
11	The design is guided by a decidability analysis of IE type constraints.
12	
13	\paragraph{Constraint decidability taxonomy.}
14	\label{sec:decidability}
15	We classify IE type constraints into three levels: (1)~\emph{context-free}: vocabulary membership and JSON structure; (2)~\emph{prefix-decidable with state}: domain/range type validity, determined at each type-bearing position given the decoded relation type and entity registry; (3)~\emph{non-local}: cross-entity type consistency, which depends on the full output.
16	Unlike code generation where types are statically declared, IE constraints fall into level~2: entity types are inferred from text, the registry is built dynamically, and constraint targets depend on the co-occurring relation.
17	OntoCoDec enforces levels 1--2 (grammar and semantic layers, respectively); level~3 is addressed by beam search or post-hoc resolution (Appendix~\ref{app:tcr}).
18	
19	\smallskip
20	\noindent
21	Existing grammar-constrained decoding frameworks (e.g., XGrammar) enforce level~1 constraints---structural validity and vocabulary membership---but IE domain/range rules require cross-position entity-type state that context-free grammars cannot express.
22	We formalize this gap:
23	
24	\smallskip
25	\noindent\textbf{Proposition 1} (Level 2 $\not\subseteq$ Level 1)\textbf{.}
26	\textit{Domain/range type constraints for IE with unbounded entity sets are not context-free.}
27	
28	\smallskip
29	\noindent\textit{Proof sketch.}
30	An output contains $n$ entities with independently chosen types, followed by a relation referencing head $e_k$ for arbitrary $k \leq n$.
31	A PDA's LIFO stack cannot retrieve position $k$ without destroying entries above it; since both $n$ and $k$ are unbounded, no PDA can enforce this constraint (formal pumping argument in Appendix~\ref{app:pumping-lemma}).
32	OntoCoDec's semantic layer addresses this with a runtime entity registry $\mathcal{D}$ (a finite map enabling random-access type lookups).\hfill$\square$
33	
34	\smallskip
35	The proof establishes theoretical impossibility for unbounded entity sets; the following paragraph addresses the finite (practical) case.
36	
37	\paragraph{Engineering applicability.}
38	When the entity set is finite, the valid language is technically regular, but the corresponding DFA requires $O(|\mathcal{E}|^{|\text{entities}|})$ states.
39	For typical SciERC documents (median 17 entities, 6 types), a DFA would require $6^{17} \approx 1.6{\times}10^{13}$ states; for DocRED (median 19 entities, 6 types), $6^{19} \approx 6.1{\times}10^{14}$ states---well beyond practical construction for online decoding.
40	The exponential scaling makes pure-grammar enforcement impractical for all but the smallest documents; OntoCoDec's registry-based approach avoids both the theoretical impossibility (unbounded) and the engineering intractability (finite but exponential).
41	
42	\noindent\textbf{Empirical Observation} (scale-dependent enabling dependency)\textbf{.} The grammar layer alone cannot enforce domain/range constraints (Proposition~1), while the semantic layer alone achieves only 24\% DR reduction at 8B without grammar-enforced vocabulary membership (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}, Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}).
43	The strength of this dependency is scale-dependent: at 70B, the semantic layer independently achieves 84\% DR reduction (\S\ref{sec:70b-results}), whereas 8B models show only 24\% independent reduction.
44	\smallskip
45	
46	We describe the problem formulation (\S\ref{sec:formulation}), each layer, and two decoding strategies (\S\ref{sec:strategies}); formal analysis of constraint composition is in Section~\ref{sec:composition}.
47	
48	
49	\subsection{Problem Formulation}
50	\label{sec:formulation}
51	
52	\paragraph{IE as structured generation.}
53	Given an input text $x$, a language model generates a structured JSON output $y$:
54	%
55	\begin{equation}\label{eq:output-schema}
56	\resizebox{.88\columnwidth}{!}{$\displaystyle
57	  y = \bigl\{\texttt{entities}: [\{n_i, \tau_i\}],\; \texttt{relations}: [\{h_j, t_j, r_j, \tau^h_j, \tau^t_j\}]\bigr\}
58	$}
59	\end{equation}
60	%
61	where each entity has a name $n_i$ and type $\tau_i \in \mathcal{E}$, and each relation specifies head $h_j$, tail $t_j$, relation type $r_j \in \mathcal{R}$, and head/tail entity types $\tau^h_j, \tau^t_j$.
62	The output JSON Schema (\S\ref{sec:grammar}) enforces a fixed field ordering (head $\to$ tail $\to$ relation $\to$ head\_type $\to$ tail\_type) via deterministic grammar compilation, ensuring the relation type $r_j$ is known before entity type fields and enabling sequential domain/range lookups (\S\ref{sec:dynamic-masking}).
63	The grammar structurally enforces entity-before-relation ordering (100\% preservation across all configurations), ensuring the entity registry (\S\ref{sec:entity-registry}) is populated before relation-level type constraints are evaluated; unregistered entities trigger domain-only filtering.
64	
65	\paragraph{Ontology compliance.}
66	Using the ontology $\mathcal{O} = (\mathcal{E}, \mathcal{R}, \sigma)$ defined in \S\ref{sec:background}, we write $\operatorname{dom}(r) = \{\tau^h : \exists \tau^t,\, (\tau^h, \tau^t) \in \sigma(r)\}$ for domain-valid head types and $\operatorname{range}(r, \tau^h) = \{\tau^t : (\tau^h, \tau^t) \in \sigma(r)\}$ for the conditional range given head type $\tau^h$.
67	Our enforcement target is \emph{pair-based} type validity: $(\tau^h, \tau^t) \in \sigma(r)$ for every triple.
68	The decoder enforces this sequentially: $\tau^h \in \operatorname{dom}(r)$ at head position, then $\tau^t \in \operatorname{range}(r, \tau^h)$ at tail position, achieving best-effort pair-based enforcement (pair validity ensured when no fallback is triggered; domain-only filtering otherwise) strictly stronger than independent domain/range checks (which over-permit 33--37\% spurious pairs on SciERC/DocRED; \S\ref{sec:rectangularity}).
69	
70	
71	\subsection{Grammar Layer}
72	\label{sec:grammar}
73	
74	A JSON Schema encoding Eq.~\eqref{eq:output-schema} with \texttt{enum} constraints ($\mathcal{E}$, $\mathcal{R}$) is compiled into a pushdown automaton via XGrammar~\cite{dong2025xgrammar}.
75	At step $t$, grammar-valid tokens $G(t) \subseteq V$ pass; all others receive logits $-\infty$, enforcing structural validity, vocabulary membership ($r_j \in \mathcal{R}$, $\tau_i \in \mathcal{E}$), but not domain/range constraints (which depend on entity types within each triple).
76	
77	\subsection{Semantic Layer}
78	\label{sec:semantic}
79	
80	The semantic layer enforces domain/range constraints through a stateful logits processor maintaining an entity registry during generation.
81	\emph{Running example}: for \textsc{Used-for} ($\operatorname{dom}{=}\{\textrm{Method}\}$, $\operatorname{range}{=}\{\textrm{Task}\}$), entity ``ResNet'' (Method) passes the head-type check while Material is blocked; ``image classification'' (Task) passes the tail-type check.
82	
83	\paragraph{Entity registry.}
84	\label{sec:entity-registry}
85	The semantic layer builds a registry $\mathcal{D}: \text{name} \to \text{type}$ incrementally during entity generation: each completed entry $\{n_i, \tau_i\}$ adds $(n_i, \tau_i)$ to $\mathcal{D}$, which is consulted during relation generation for type lookups.
86	The registry uses \emph{first-seen} semantics: when an entity name appears in multiple triples, the type assigned at its first occurrence governs all subsequent constraint checks, making type enforcement sensitive to generation order.
87	At each step $t$, the processor pattern-matches the decoded prefix to identify the current field position; semantic masks activate only at type-bearing positions (\textsc{HeadType}, \textsc{TailType}).
88	\label{sec:position-detection}
89	
90	\paragraph{Dynamic masking.}
91	\label{sec:dynamic-masking}
92	At type-bearing positions, the processor computes a semantic mask $S(t) \subseteq V$.
93	At a \textsc{HeadType} position, if $h_j \in \mathcal{D}$ and $\mathcal{D}[h_j] \in \operatorname{dom}(r_j)$, the mask allows only the registered type; otherwise it falls back to all domain-valid types $\operatorname{dom}(r_j)$.
94	At a \textsc{TailType} position, the mask restricts to $\operatorname{range}(r_j, \tau^h_j)$ conditioned on the already-determined head type, further narrowing to the registered type when $t_j \in \mathcal{D}$.
95	This sequential check achieves best-effort pair-based enforcement (\S\ref{sec:formulation}), strictly tighter than independent domain/range checking; ``best-effort'' reflects the grammar-first fallback (\S\ref{sec:composition}), which prioritizes JSON integrity over type validity when constraints conflict.
96	
97	\paragraph{Tokenizer-agnostic type masking.}
98	We use \emph{continuation masking} to handle context-dependent tokenizations and prefix-shared types (e.g., \textsc{Sen}/\textsc{Sid}/\textsc{Side} in WikiEvents): a compile-time prefix map identifies valid continuations for each type name, disambiguating over multiple steps across byte-level BPE (Qwen, Llama) and SentencePiece (Mistral).
99	
100	\paragraph{Grammar-semantic interaction.}
101	\label{sec:interaction}
102	The final mask is $G(t) \cap S(t)$; when empty, grammar-first fallback applies (\S\ref{sec:composition}), embodying this interaction: semantic constraints reduce DR by only 24\% without grammar-enforced vocabulary.
103	
104	
105	\subsection{Decoding Strategies}
106	\label{sec:strategies}
107	
108	\paragraph{S1 (joint extraction): Single-pass decoding.}
109	Both layers are active throughout generation; the entity registry populates during entity decoding and is immediately available for relation-level type checking.
110	
111	\paragraph{S2 (two-pass pipeline): Two-pass decoding.}\label{sec:s2}
112	A first pass extracts entities; these compile an input-dependent grammar for the second pass.
113	S1 achieves higher R-F1 at comparable DR (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}) and is the primary configuration.
114	
```

```json
      "content": "% Section 3 — Method (Draft)\n% OntoCoDec: Type-Valid Information Extraction\n% via Compiled Ontology Constraints\n\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\input{fig1_framework}\n\nWe present OntoCoDec, a framework that translates IE ontologies into token-level constraints enforced during autoregressive generation.\nThe design is guided by a decidability analysis of IE type constraints.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint decidability taxonomy.}\n\\label{sec:decidability}\nWe classify IE type constraints into three levels: (1)~\\emph{context-free}: vocabulary membership and JSON structure; (2)~\\emph{prefix-decidable with state}: domain/range type validity, determined at each type-bearing position given the decoded relation type and entity registry; (3)~\\emph{non-local}: cross-entity type consistency, which depends on the full output.\nUnlike code generation where types are statically declared, IE constraints fall into level~2: entity types are inferred from text, the registry is built dynamically, and constraint targets depend on the co-occurring relation.\nOntoCoDec enforces levels 1--2 (grammar and semantic layers, respectively); level~3 is addressed by beam search or post-hoc resolution (Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}).\n\n\\smallskip\n\\noindent\nExisting grammar-constrained decoding frameworks (e.g., XGrammar) enforce level~1 constraints---structural validity and vocabulary membership---but IE domain/range rules require cross-position entity-type state that context-free grammars cannot express.\nWe formalize this gap:\n\n\\smallskip\n\\noindent\\textbf{Proposition 1} (Level 2 $\\not\\subseteq$ Level 1)\\textbf{.}\n\\textit{Domain/range type constraints for IE with unbounded entity sets are not context-free.}\n\n\\smallskip\n\\noindent\\textit{Proof sketch.}\nAn output contains $n$ entities with independently chosen types, followed by a relation referencing head $e_k$ for arbitrary $k \\leq n$.\nA PDA's LIFO stack cannot retrieve position $k$ without destroying entries above it; since both $n$ and $k$ are unbounded, no PDA can enforce this constraint (formal pumping argument in Appendix~\\ref{app:pumping-lemma}).\nOntoCoDec's semantic layer addresses this with a runtime entity registry $\\mathcal{D}$ (a finite map enabling random-access type lookups).\\hfill$\\square$\n\n\\smallskip\nThe proof establishes theoretical impossibility for unbounded entity sets; the following paragraph addresses the finite (practical) case.\n\n\\paragraph{Engineering applicability.}\nWhen the entity set is finite, the valid language is technically regular, but the corresponding DFA requires $O(|\\mathcal{E}|^{|\\text{entities}|})$ states.\nFor typical SciERC documents (median 17 entities, 6 types), a DFA would require $6^{17} \\approx 1.6{\\times}10^{13}$ states; for DocRED (median 19 entities, 6 types), $6^{19} \\approx 6.1{\\times}10^{14}$ states---well beyond practical construction for online decoding.\nThe exponential scaling makes pure-grammar enforcement impractical for all but the smallest documents; OntoCoDec's registry-based approach avoids both the theoretical impossibility (unbounded) and the engineering intractability (finite but exponential).\n\n\\noindent\\textbf{Empirical Observation} (scale-dependent enabling dependency)\\textbf{.} The grammar layer alone cannot enforce domain/range constraints (Proposition~1), while the semantic layer alone achieves only 24\\% DR reduction at 8B without grammar-enforced vocabulary membership (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}, Table~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}).\nThe strength of this dependency is scale-dependent: at 70B, the semantic layer independently achieves 84\\% DR reduction (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}), whereas 8B models show only 24\\% independent reduction.\n\\smallskip\n\nWe describe the problem formulation (\\S\\ref{sec:formulation}), each layer, and two decoding strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:strategies}); formal analysis of constraint composition is in Section~\\ref{sec:composition}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:formulation}\n\n\\paragraph{IE as structured generation.}\nGiven an input text $x$, a language model generates a structured JSON output $y$:\n%\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:output-schema}\n\\resizebox{.88\\columnwidth}{!}{$\\displaystyle\n  y = \\bigl\\{\\texttt{entities}: [\\{n_i, \\tau_i\\}],\\; \\texttt{relations}: [\\{h_j, t_j, r_j, \\tau^h_j, \\tau^t_j\\}]\\bigr\\}\n$}\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere each entity has a name $n_i$ and type $\\tau_i \\in \\mathcal{E}$, and each relation specifies head $h_j$, tail $t_j$, relation type $r_j \\in \\mathcal{R}$, and head/tail entity types $\\tau^h_j, \\tau^t_j$.\nThe output JSON Schema (\\S\\ref{sec:grammar}) enforces a fixed field ordering (head $\\to$ tail $\\to$ relation $\\to$ head\\_type $\\to$ tail\\_type) via deterministic grammar compilation, ensuring the relation type $r_j$ is known before entity type fields and enabling sequential domain/range lookups (\\S\\ref{sec:dynamic-masking}).\nThe grammar structurally enforces entity-before-relation ordering (100\\% preservation across all configurations), ensuring the entity registry (\\S\\ref{sec:entity-registry}) is populated before relation-level type constraints are evaluated; unregistered entities trigger domain-only filtering.\n\n\\paragraph{Ontology compliance.}\nUsing the ontology $\\mathcal{O} = (\\mathcal{E}, \\mathcal{R}, \\sigma)$ defined in \\S\\ref{sec:background}, we write $\\operatorname{dom}(r) = \\{\\tau^h : \\exists \\tau^t,\\, (\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)\\}$ for domain-valid head types and $\\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h) = \\{\\tau^t : (\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)\\}$ for the conditional range given head type $\\tau^h$.\nOur enforcement target is \\emph{pair-based} type validity: $(\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)$ for every triple.\nThe decoder enforces this sequentially: $\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r)$ at head position, then $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h)$ at tail position, achieving best-effort pair-based enforcement (pair validity ensured when no fallback is triggered; domain-only filtering otherwise) strictly stronger than independent domain/range checks (which over-permit 33--37\\% spurious pairs on SciERC/DocRED; \\S\\ref{sec:rectangularity}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Grammar Layer}\n\\label{sec:grammar}\n\nA JSON Schema encoding Eq.~\\eqref{eq:output-schema} with \\texttt{enum} constraints ($\\mathcal{E}$, $\\mathcal{R}$) is compiled into a pushdown automaton via XGrammar~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar}.\nAt step $t$, grammar-valid tokens $G(t) \\subseteq V$ pass; all others receive logits $-\\infty$, enforcing structural validity, vocabulary membership ($r_j \\in \\mathcal{R}$, $\\tau_i \\in \\mathcal{E}$), but not domain/range constraints (which depend on entity types within each triple).\n\n\\subsection{Semantic Layer}\n\\label{sec:semantic}\n\nThe semantic layer enforces domain/range constraints through a stateful logits processor maintaining an entity registry during generation.\n\\emph{Running example}: for \\textsc{Used-for} ($\\operatorname{dom}{=}\\{\\textrm{Method}\\}$, $\\operatorname{range}{=}\\{\\textrm{Task}\\}$), entity ``ResNet'' (Method) passes the head-type check while Material is blocked; ``image classification'' (Task) passes the tail-type check.\n\n\\paragraph{Entity registry.}\n\\label{sec:entity-registry}\nThe semantic layer builds a registry $\\mathcal{D}: \\text{name} \\to \\text{type}$ incrementally during entity generation: each completed entry $\\{n_i, \\tau_i\\}$ adds $(n_i, \\tau_i)$ to $\\mathcal{D}$, which is consulted during relation generation for type lookups.\nThe registry uses \\emph{first-seen} semantics: when an entity name appears in multiple triples, the type assigned at its first occurrence governs all subsequent constraint checks, making type enforcement sensitive to generation order.\nAt each step $t$, the processor pattern-matches the decoded prefix to identify the current field position; semantic masks activate only at type-bearing positions (\\textsc{HeadType}, \\textsc{TailType}).\n\\label{sec:position-detection}\n\n\\paragraph{Dynamic masking.}\n\\label{sec:dynamic-masking}\nAt type-bearing positions, the processor computes a semantic mask $S(t) \\subseteq V$.\nAt a \\textsc{HeadType} position, if $h_j \\in \\mathcal{D}$ and $\\mathcal{D}[h_j] \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r_j)$, the mask allows only the registered type; otherwise it falls back to all domain-valid types $\\operatorname{dom}(r_j)$.\nAt a \\textsc{TailType} position, the mask restricts to $\\operatorname{range}(r_j, \\tau^h_j)$ conditioned on the already-determined head type, further narrowing to the registered type when $t_j \\in \\mathcal{D}$.\nThis sequential check achieves best-effort pair-based enforcement (\\S\\ref{sec:formulation}), strictly tighter than independent domain/range checking; ``best-effort'' reflects the grammar-first fallback (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}), which prioritizes JSON integrity over type validity when constraints conflict.\n\n\\paragraph{Tokenizer-agnostic type masking.}\nWe use \\emph{continuation masking} to handle context-dependent tokenizations and prefix-shared types (e.g., \\textsc{Sen}/\\textsc{Sid}/\\textsc{Side} in WikiEvents): a compile-time prefix map identifies valid continuations for each type name, disambiguating over multiple steps across byte-level BPE (Qwen, Llama) and SentencePiece (Mistral).\n\n\\paragraph{Grammar-semantic interaction.}\n\\label{sec:interaction}\nThe final mask is $G(t) \\cap S(t)$; when empty, grammar-first fallback applies (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}), embodying this interaction: semantic constraints reduce DR by only 24\\% without grammar-enforced vocabulary.\n\n\n\\subsection{Decoding Strategies}\n\\label{sec:strategies}\n\n\\paragraph{S1 (joint extraction): Single-pass decoding.}\nBoth layers are active throughout generation; the entity registry populates during entity decoding and is immediately available for relation-level type checking.\n\n\\paragraph{S2 (two-pass pipeline): Two-pass decoding.}\\label{sec:s2}\nA first pass extracts entities; these compile an input-dependent grammar for the second pass.\nS1 achieves higher R-F1 at comparable DR (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}) and is the primary configuration.\n",
```

```text
2:% exp_ids: gollie_7b_scierc_zs_100, ontcodec_qwen_ft_s1_scierc_100, ontcodec_llama_ft_s1_scierc_100
4:\section{GoLLIE Comparison}
5:\label{app:gollie-comparison}
7:GoLLIE~\cite{sainz2024gollie} and OntoCoDec represent complementary paradigms for schema compliance in IE:
10:\item \emph{Training-time internalization} (GoLLIE, KnowCoder): Schema knowledge is encoded into model parameters via annotation-guideline-aware training. This achieves compliance through learned generation patterns but cannot guarantee zero violations at inference time, and requires schema-specific retraining when ontologies evolve.
14:The two approaches are orthogonal: OntoCoDec can be composed with GoLLIE or similar training-based systems to provide runtime enforcement guarantees atop learned schema compliance.
```

_source line 8493 · 2026-05-23T12:20:30.816Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01UT1rHmypESYvYJ7oVmvi3v`</summary>

```text
1	% §5 Setup (compressed from Violation Landscape)
2	% exp_ids: mvp_phase0_violation_rate, mvp_phase0_scierc_schema,
3	%          mvp_phase0_violation_rate_docred, mvp_phase0_docred_schema
4	
5	\label{sec:violation_landscape}
6	
7	We evaluate OntoCoDec in two deployment configurations: greedy S1 (DR\,$\leq$\,0.21\%, $+$10\% latency) and beam S1 (all violations\,$\to$\,0\%, 1.39$\times$ latency); zero-shot evaluation (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-zs}) serves as a diagnostic probe for the fine-tuned setting (R-F1 40--46\%).
8	
9	\paragraph{Setup.}
10	We evaluate on SciERC~\cite{luan2018scierc} (551 test sentences, 7 relations, $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.333$) and DocRED~\cite{yao2019docred} (998 development documents; test annotations are not publicly available; 96 relations, $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$).
11	RE type constraints are extracted from observed type pairs in training data (neither dataset provides a formal ontology); EE constraints on WikiEvents (\S\ref{sec:wikievents}) derive from the KAIROS ontology.
12	All constraints are schema-level, independent of evaluation splits.
13	Primary model: Qwen3-8B~\cite{qwen2025qwen3}, zero-shot greedy decoding; cross-model validation in Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-zs}, composability with fine-tuning in \S\ref{sec:composability}.
14	LoRA hyperparameters (rank, $\alpha$, learning rate) were tuned on SciERC and transferred to DocRED without dataset-specific optimization; given the substantial schema complexity difference (7 vs.\ 96 relations), this transfer may underestimate DocRED fine-tuning potential.
15	Our R-F1 range (40--46\% under LoRA) is consistent with recent LLM-based generative RE evaluations on these benchmarks, where document-level closed IE without gold entity spans typically achieves 35--50\%~\cite{wadhwa2023revisiting}; supervised span-based methods operating under different assumptions (gold entity spans, dedicated architectures) are not directly comparable.
16	DocRED development documents were used for evaluation only.
17	Four violation categories: \textbf{DR} (type pair $\notin \sigma(r)$), \textbf{RV} (relation $\notin \mathcal{R}$), \textbf{ET} (entity type $\notin \mathcal{E}$), \textbf{CF} (conflicting types across triples).
18	Baseline DR: 15.37\% (SciERC), 18.46\% (DocRED); schema-informed prompting reduces DR by 62\% but is per-relation unreliable (Appendix~\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).
19	
```

```text
65	
66	% === Limitations (ARR requirement: standalone section, does not count toward page limit) ===
67	\clearpage
68	\section*{Limitations}
69	
70	\paragraph{Constraint scope.}
71	CF is an architectural consequence of per-triple independent enforcement: CF$\leftrightarrow$DR represents a fundamental trade-off (eliminating CF re-routes violations to DR; Appendix~\ref{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}), and its impact is model-dependent (S1 introduces CF for calibrated models but reduces it for poorly-calibrated ones).
72	Global entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF.
73	Entity-first generation order is an architectural constraint: the grammar enforces entities before relations, ensuring the registry is populated before type checks; alternative orderings would require deferred constraint evaluation.
74	Residual DR under fine-tuning (36 violations total) decomposes into entity type errors (39\%), schema coverage gaps (33\%), and model generation errors (28\%); schema gaps arise from training-data-derived constraints that may not cover all valid type pairs (Appendix~\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).
75	Our empirical constraints are derived from training data type-pair co-occurrences; schema evolution (adding new entity types or relations) would require constraint regeneration, and the 0.6--1.0\% uncovered valid type pairs suggest potential over-restriction during transition periods.
76	We do not decompose CF violations into first-seen cascading errors versus legitimate type ambiguity; this decomposition requires gold entity type annotations at the mention level, which SciERC provides only at the entity level.
77	Post-hoc type consensus re-voting eliminates all CF in our experiments, at the cost of DR regression (up to $+$9.38\,pp on DocRED zero-shot; Appendix~\ref{app:tcr}); integrating consensus into the decoding loop remains future work.
78	
79	\paragraph{Evaluation coverage.}
80	Zero-shot evaluation covers three 8B-class and one 70B-class model; fine-tuning composability is validated on two 8B-class models (full datasets) and one 70B-class model (SciERC full, DocRED 200-document subset).
81	Scaling to smaller models has not been verified; the schema restrictiveness--enforcement difficulty relationship is based on three schemas.
82	Evaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); LoRA hyperparameters were tuned on SciERC and transferred to DocRED without dataset-specific optimization, which may underestimate DocRED potential.
83	WikiEvents fine-tuning is preliminary ($n{=}20$); DocRED uses the development set (test annotations unavailable).
84	All main experiments use greedy decoding; beam search compatibility is confirmed but comprehensive evaluation across all configurations remains future work (Appendices~\ref{app:sampling},~\ref{app:beam-search}).
85	
86	\paragraph{Engineering considerations.}
87	Our approach requires access to model logits, excluding API-only models; continuation masking assumes distinguishable token prefixes per type name, which may be challenged by morphologically similar types (e.g., \texttt{Protein} vs.\ \texttt{Protein\_Complex}; Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}).
88	70B experiments use AWQ INT4 quantization; an 8B ablation confirms enforcement is unaffected (Appendix~\ref{app:quantization}), though a direct 70B FP16 comparison exceeds our hardware capacity.
89	Evaluation is limited to English-language datasets; the mechanism is language-independent but CJK tokenization fragmentation may increase masking steps (Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}).
```

</details>

```json
    "originalFile": "% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity necessarily carries a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.\nWe call such outputs \\emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.\nViolations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\\% of triples on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\\% on DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 0.5--2\\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates follow an observed pattern consistent with schema restrictiveness (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.\nAt competitive quality (R-F1 40--46\\%), even 1--2\\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption, making reliable constraint enforcement necessary.\n\nExisting approaches leave a fundamental gap.\nSchema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \\textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \\emph{increase} by 5.8\\,pp).\nGrammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} ensures syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.\nPost-hoc filtering discards up to 74\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\nTraining-based approaches~\\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} internalize schema knowledge but provide only soft guidance without runtime enforcement.\n\nWe propose \\textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.\nA decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \\emph{grammar layer} compiles ontology constraints into context-free grammars (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \\emph{semantic layer} interprets type constraints at runtime via dynamic logit masking over an entity registry, with a scale-dependent layer interaction: at 8B, the semantic layer achieves only 24\\% independent DR reduction (near-complete when combined), while at 70B it reaches 84\\% independently.\nOntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and is applicable to autoregressive LMs with logits access; we validate on three English datasets (up to 96 relation types), three 8B-class models across three tokenizer families, with 70B-class validation (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}).\nLoRA + greedy S1 reduces DR to near-zero: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\% at R-F1 40--46\\% ($\\pm$0.3\\,pp, $+$10\\% latency); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types at 8B on both RE datasets under fine-tuning (1.39$\\times$ latency).\nDR enforcement converts ontologically invalid violations to individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\% fine-tuned); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED, with total violations decreasing $\\sim$31\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\n\nOntoCoDec is a constraint enforcement method, orthogonal and complementary to training-based schema-aware approaches such as GoLLIE~\\cite{sainz2024gollie} and KnowCoder~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}; it can be composed with any generative IE system to improve schema compliance without retraining.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]\n    \\item A two-layer constrained decoding framework grounded in the observation that domain/range type constraints require stateful enforcement beyond context-free grammars---grammar-only approaches face state spaces of $6^{17}$--$6^{19}$ for typical IE schemas (Proposition~1; {
```
```json
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section3_method_draft.tex",
  "old_string": "Existing grammar-constrained decoding frameworks (e.g., XGrammar) enforce level~1 constraints---structural validity and vocabulary membership---but IE domain/range rules require cross-position entity-type state that context-free grammars cannot express.\nWe formalize this gap:\n\n\\smallskip\n\\noindent\\textbf{Proposition 1} (Level 2 $\\not\\subseteq$ Level 1)\\textbf{.}\n\\textit{Domain/range type constraints for IE with unbounded entity sets are not context-free.}\n\n\\smallskip\n\\noindent\\textit{Proof sketch.}\nAn output contains $n$ entities with independently chosen types, followed by a relation referencing head $e_k$ for arbitrary $k \\leq n$.\nA PDA's LIFO stack cannot retrieve position $k$ without destroying entries above it; since both $n$ and $k$ are unbounded, no PDA can enforce this constraint (formal pumping argument in Appendix~\\ref{app:pumping-lemma}).\nOntoCoDec's semantic layer addresses this with a runtime entity registry $\\mathcal{D}$ (a finite map enabling random-access type lookups).\\hfill$\\square$\n\n\\smallskip\nThe proof establishes theoretical impossibility for unbounded entity sets; the following paragraph addresses the finite (practical) case.\n\n\\paragraph{Engineering applicability.}\nWhen the entity set is finite, the valid language is technically regular, but the corresponding DFA requires $O(|\\mathcal{E}|^{|\\text{entities}|})$ states.\nFor typical SciERC documents (median 17 entities, 6 types), a DFA would require $6^{17} \\approx 1.6{\\times}10^{13}$ states; for DocRED (median 19 entities, 6 types), $6^{19} \\approx 6.1{\\times}10^{14}$ states---well beyond practical construction for online decoding.\nThe exponential scaling makes pure-grammar enforcement impractical for all but the smallest documents; OntoCoDec's registry-based approach avoids both the theoretical impossibility (unbounded) and the engineering intractability (finite but exponential).",
  "new_string": "Existing grammar-constrained decoding frameworks (e.g., XGrammar) enforce level~1 constraints---structural validity and vocabulary membership---but IE domain/range rules require cross-position entity-type state that context-free grammars cannot express.\nEven when the entity set is finite, encoding type constraints into a DFA requires $O(|\\mathcal{E}|^{|\\text{entities}|})$ states: for typical SciERC documents (median 17 entities, 6 types), $6^{17} \\approx 1.6{\\times}10^{13}$ states; for DocRED (median 19 entities, 6 types), $6^{19} \\approx 6.1{\\times}10^{14}$---well beyond practical construction for online decoding.\nFormally, domain/range constraints for unbounded entity sets are not context-free (\\textbf{Proposition~1}; proof via pumping argument in Appendix~\\ref{app:pumping-lemma}): a PDA cannot retrieve the type of entity $e_k$ without destroying stack entries above it.\nOntoCoDec's semantic layer addresses this with a runtime entity registry $\\mathcal{D}$ (a finite map enabling random-access type lookups), avoiding both the theoretical impossibility (unbounded) and the engineering intractability (finite but exponential)."
}
```

```json
    "newString": "Existing grammar-constrained decoding frameworks (e.g., XGrammar) enforce level~1 constraints---structural validity and vocabulary membership---but IE domain/range rules require cross-position entity-type state that context-free grammars cannot express.\nEven when the entity set is finite, encoding type constraints into a DFA requires $O(|\\mathcal{E}|^{|\\text{entities}|})$ states: for typical SciERC documents (median 17 entities, 6 types), $6^{17} \\approx 1.6{\\times}10^{13}$ states; for DocRED (median 19 entities, 6 types), $6^{19} \\approx 6.1{\\times}10^{14}$---well beyond practical construction for online decoding.\nFormally, domain/range constraints for unbounded entity sets are not context-free (\\textbf{Proposition~1}; proof via pumping argument in Appendix~\\ref{app:pumping-lemma}): a PDA cannot retrieve the type of entity $e_k$ without destroying stack entries above it.\nOntoCoDec's semantic layer addresses this with a runtime entity registry $\\mathcal{D}$ (a finite map enabling random-access type lookups), avoiding both the theoretical impossibility (unbounded) and the engineering intractability (finite but exponential).",
    "originalFile": "% Section 3 — Method (Draft)\n% OntoCoDec: Type-Valid Information Extraction\n% via Compiled Ontology Constraints\n\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\input{fig1_framework}\n\nWe present OntoCoDec, a framework that translates IE ontologies into token-level constraints enforced during autoregressive generation.\nThe design is guided by a decidability analysis of IE type constraints.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint decidability taxonomy.}\n\\label{sec:decidability}\nWe classify IE type constraints into three levels: (1)~\\emph{context-free}: vocabulary membership and JSON structure; (2)~\\emph{prefix-decidable with state}: domain/range type validity, determined at each type-bearing position given the decoded relation type and entity registry; (3)~\\emph{non-local}: cross-entity type consistency, which depends on the full output.\nUnlike code generation where types are statically declared, IE constraints fall into level~2: entity types are inferred from text, the registry is built dynamically, and constraint targets depend on the co-occurring relation.\nOntoCoDec enforces levels 1--2 (grammar and semantic layers, respectively); level~3 is addressed by beam search or post-hoc resolution (Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}).\n\n\\smallskip\n\\noindent\nExisting grammar-constrained decoding frameworks (e.g., XGrammar) enforce level~1 constraints---structural validity and vocabulary membership---but IE domain/range rules require cross-position entity-type state that context-free grammars cannot express.\nWe formalize this gap:\n\n\\smallskip\n\\noindent\\textbf{Proposition 1} (Level 2 $\\not\\subseteq$ Level 1)\\textbf{.}\n\\textit{Domain/range type constraints for IE with unbounded entity sets are not context-free.}\n\n\\smallskip\n\\noindent\\textit{Proof sketch.}\nAn output contains $n$ entities with independently chosen types, followed by a relation referencing head $e_k$ for arbitrary $k \\leq n$.\nA PDA's LIFO stack cannot retrieve position $k$ without destroying entries above it; since both $n$ and $k$ are unbounded, no PDA can enforce this constraint (formal pumping argument in Appendix~\\ref{app:pumping-lemma}).\nOntoCoDec's semantic layer addresses this with a runtime entity registry $\\mathcal{D}$ (a finite map enabling random-access type lookups).\\hfill$\\square$\n\n\\smallskip\nThe proof establishes theoretical impossibility for unbounded entity sets; the following paragraph addresses the finite (practical) case.\n\n\\paragraph{Engineering applicability.}\nWhen the entity set is finite, the valid language is technically regular, but the corresponding DFA requires $O(|\\mathcal{E}|^{|\\text{entities}|})$ states.\nFor typical SciERC documents (median 17 entities, 6 types), a DFA would require $6^{17} \\approx 1.6{\\times}10^{13}$ states; for DocRED (median 19 entities, 6 types), $6^{19} \\approx 6.1{\\times}10^{14}$ states---well beyond practical construction for online decoding.\nThe exponential scaling makes pure-grammar enforcement impractical for all but the smallest documents; OntoCoDec's registry-based approach avoids both the theoretical impossibility (unbounded) and the engineering intractability (finite but exponential).\n\n\\noindent\\textbf{Empirical Observation} (scale-dependent enabling dependency)\\textbf{.} The grammar layer alone cannot enforce domain/range constraints (Proposition~1), while the semantic layer alone achieves only 24\\% DR reduction at 8B without grammar-enforced vocabulary membership (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}, Table~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}).\nThe strength of this dependency is scale-dependent: at 70B, the semantic layer independently achieves 84\\% DR reduction (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}), whereas 8B models show only 24\\% independent reduction.\n\\smallskip\n\nWe describe the problem formulation (\\S\\ref{sec:formulation}), each layer, and two decoding strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:strategies}); formal analysis of constraint composition is in Section~\\ref{sec:composition}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:formulation}\n\n\\paragraph{IE as structured generation.}\nGiven an input text $x$, a language model generates a structured JSON output $y$:\n%\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:output-schema}\n\\resizebox{.88\\columnwidth}{!}{$\\displaystyle\n  y = \\bigl\\{\\texttt{entities}: [\\{n_i, \\tau_i\\}],\\; \\texttt{relations}: [\\{h_j, t_j, r_j, \\tau^h_j, \\tau^t_j\\}]\\bigr\\}\n$}\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere each entity has a name $n_i$ and type $\\tau_i \\in \\mathcal{E}$, and each relation specifies head $h_j$, tail $t_j$, relation type $r_j \\in \\mathcal{R}$, and head/tail entity types $\\tau^h_j, \\tau^t_j$.\nThe output JSON Schema (\\S\\ref{sec:grammar}) enforces a fixed field ordering (head $\\to$ tail $\\to$ relation $\\to$ head\\_type $\\to$ tail\\_type) via deterministic grammar compilation, ensuring the relation type $r_j$ is known before entity type fields and enabling sequential domain/range lookups (\\S\\ref{sec:dynamic-masking}).\nThe grammar structurally enforces entity-before-relation ordering (100\\% preservation across all configurations), ensuring the entity registry (\\S\\ref{sec:entity-registry}) is populated before relation-level type constraints are evaluated; unregistered entities trigger domain-only filtering.\n\n\\paragraph{Ontology compliance.}\nUsing the ontology $\\mathcal{O} = (\\mathcal{E}, \\mathcal{R}, \\sigma)$ defined in \\S\\ref{sec:background}, we write $\\operatorname{dom}(r) = \\{\\tau^h : \\exists \\tau^t,\\, (\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)\\}$ for domain-valid head types and $\\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h) = \\{\\tau^t : (\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)\\}$ for the conditional range given head type $\\tau^h$.\nOur enforcement target is \\emph{pair-based} type validity: $(\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)$ for every triple.\nThe decoder enforces this sequentially: $\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r)$ at head position, then $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h)$ at tail position, achieving best-effort pair-based enforcement (pair validity ensured when no fallback is triggered; domain-only filtering otherwise) strictly stronger than independent domain/range checks (which over-permit 33--37\\% spurious pairs on SciERC/DocRED; \\S\\ref{sec:rectangularity}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Grammar Layer}\n\\label{sec:grammar}\n\nA JSON Schema encoding Eq.~\\eqref{eq:output-schema} with \\texttt{enum} constraints ($\\mathcal{E}$, $\\mathcal{R}$) is compiled into a pushdown automaton via XGrammar~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar}.\nAt step $t$, grammar-valid tokens $G(t) \\subseteq V$ pass; all others receive logits $-\\infty$, enforcing structural validity, vocabulary membership ($r_j \\in \\mathcal{R}$, $\\tau_i \\in \\mathcal{E}$), but not domain/range constraints (which depend on entity types within each triple).\n\n\\subsection{Semantic Layer}\n\\label{sec:semantic}\n\nThe semantic layer enforces domain/range constraints through a stateful logits processor maintaining an entity registry during generation.\n\\emph{Running example}: for \\textsc{Used-for} ($\\operatorname{dom}{=}\\{\\textrm{Method}\\}$, $\\operatorname{range}{=}\\{\\textrm{Task}\\}$), entity ``ResNet'' (Method) passes the head-type check while Material is blocked; ``image classification'' (Task) passes the tail-type check.\n\n\\paragraph{Entity registry.}\n\\label{sec:entity-registry}\nThe semantic layer builds a registry $\\mathcal{D}: \\text{name} \\to \\text{type}$ incrementally during entity generation: each completed entry $\\{n_i, \\tau_i\\}$ adds $(n_i, \\tau_i)$ to $\\mathcal{D}$, which is consulted during relation generation for type lookups.\nThe registry uses \\emph{first-seen} semantics: when an entity name appears in multiple triples, the type assigned at its first occurrence governs all subsequent constraint checks, making type enforcement sensitive to generation order.\nAt each step $t$, the processor pattern-matches the decoded prefix to identify the current field position; semantic masks activate only at type-bearing positions (\\textsc{HeadType}, \\textsc{TailType}).\n\\label{sec:position-detection}\n\n\\paragraph{Dynamic masking.}\n\\label{sec:dynamic-masking}\nAt type-bearing positions, the processor computes a semantic mask $S(t) \\subseteq V$.\nAt a \\textsc{HeadType} position, if $h_j \\in \\mathcal{D}$ and $\\mathcal{D}[h_j] \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r_j)$, the mask allows only the registered type; otherwise it falls back to all domain-valid types $\\operatorname{dom}(r_j)$.\nAt a \\textsc{TailType} position, the mask restricts to $\\operatorname{range}(r_j, \\tau^h_j)$ conditioned on the already-determined head type, further narrowing to the registered type when $t_j \\in \\mathcal{D}$.\nThis sequential check achieves best-effort pair-based enforcement (\\S\\ref{sec:formulation}), strictly tighter than independent domain/range checking; ``best-effort'' reflects the grammar-first fallback (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}), which prioritizes JSON integrity over type validity when constraints conflict.\n\n\\paragraph{Tokenizer-agnostic type masking.}\nWe use \\emph{continuation masking} to handle context-dependent tokenizations and prefix-shared types (e.g., \\textsc{Sen}/\\textsc{Sid}/\\textsc{Side} in WikiEvents): a compile-time prefix map identifies valid continuations for each type name, disambiguating over multiple steps across byte-level BPE (Qwen, Llama) and SentencePiece (Mistral).\n\n\\paragraph{Grammar-semantic interaction.}\n\\label{sec:interaction}\nThe final mask is $G(t) \\cap S(t)$; when empty, grammar-first fallback applies (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}), embodying this interaction: semantic constraints reduce DR by only 24\\% without grammar-enforced vocabulary.\n\n\n\\subsection{Decoding Strategies}\n\\label{sec:strategies}\n\n\\paragraph{S1 (joint extraction): Single-pass decoding.}\nBoth layers are active throughout generation; the entity registry populates during entity decoding and is immediately available for relation-level type checking.\n\n\\paragraph{S2 (two-pass pipeline): Two-pass decoding.}\\label{sec:s2}\nA first pass extracts entities; these compile an input-dependent grammar for the second pass.\nS1 achieves higher R-F1 at comparable DR (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}) and is the primary configuration.\n",

          "-An output contains $n$ entities with independently chosen types, followed by a relation referencing head $e_k$ for arbitrary $k \\leq n$.",
          "-A PDA's LIFO stack cannot retrieve position $k$ without destroying entries above it; since both $n$ and $k$ are unbounded, no PDA can enforce this constraint (formal pumping argument in Appendix~\\ref{app:pumping-lemma}).",
          "-OntoCoDec's semantic layer addresses this with a runtime entity registry $\\mathcal{D}$ (a finite map enabling random-access type lookups).\\hfill$\\square$",
          "-",
          "-\\smallskip",
          "-The proof establishes theoretical impossibility for unbounded entity sets; the following paragraph addresses the finite (practical) case.",
          "-",
          "-\\paragraph{Engineering applicability.}",
          "-When the entity set is finite, the valid language is technically regular, but the corresponding DFA requires $O(|\\mathcal{E}|^{|\\text{entities}|})$ states.",
          "-For typical SciERC documents (median 17 entities, 6 types), a DFA would require $6^{17} \\approx 1.6{\\times}10^{13}$ states; for DocRED (median 19 entities, 6 types), $6^{19} \\approx 6.1{\\times}10^{14}$ states---well beyond practical construction for online decoding.",
          "-The exponential scaling makes pure-grammar enforcement impractical for all but the smallest documents; OntoCoDec's registry-based approach avoids both the theoretical impossibility (unbounded) and the engineering intractability (finite but exponential).",
          "-",
          " \\noindent\\textbf{Empirical Observation} (scale-dependent enabling dependency)\\textbf{.} The grammar layer alone cannot enforce domain/range constraints (Proposition~1), while the semantic layer alone achieves only 24\\% DR reduction at 8B without grammar-enforced vocabulary membership (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}, Table~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}).",
          " The strength of this dependency is scale-dependent: at 70B, the semantic layer independently achieves 84\\% DR reduction (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}), whereas 8B models show only 24\\% independent reduction.",
          " \\smallskip"
        ]
      }
    ],
```
