{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section7_conclusion_compressed.tex",
  "old_string": "OntoCoDec compiles IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints, validated across 3 datasets, 3+ models, and 2 tasks.\nBecause IE type constraints exceed context-free expressiveness (Proposition~1), the semantic layer bridges the gap grammar-only enforcement cannot address (3\\% vs.\\ 94\\% DR reduction at 8B), using constructive forward masking over a dynamic entity registry.\nThis scale-dependent interaction (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results})---likely reflecting improved vocabulary calibration at larger scale, where 70B models assign higher probability to ontology-valid tokens (Appendix~\\ref{app:causal-tracing})---suggests grammar-constrained decoding is necessary but increasingly insufficient as models improve; constraint enforcement is preserved under INT4 quantization (4$\\times$ compression; Appendix~\\ref{app:quantization}).",
  "new_string": "OntoCoDec compiles IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints, validated across 3 datasets, 3+ models, and 2 tasks.\nThe central empirical finding is non-additive layer interaction: grammar alone achieves 3\\% DR reduction on SciERC, semantic alone 24\\%, but their composition yields 94\\% at 8B (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results})---an enabling dependency where grammar-enforced vocabulary membership creates the clean input the semantic layer requires.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale-dependent layer interaction.}\nThis dependency weakens at scale: at 70B, the semantic layer independently achieves 84\\% DR reduction (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}).\nWe hypothesize this reflects improved vocabulary calibration: larger models assign higher probability to ontology-valid tokens, reducing the semantic layer's dependence on grammar-enforced vocabulary membership (Appendix~\\ref{app:causal-tracing}).\nSupporting evidence includes the fallback frequency gap: under LoRA + S1, Qwen-8B triggers 0 grammar-first fallbacks on SciERC while Llama-8B triggers 5{,}882 (Table~\\ref{tab:ft-ablation}), yet both achieve DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\%, confirming that fallback frequency does not determine enforcement effectiveness.\nGrammar-constrained decoding is thus necessary but increasingly insufficient as models improve; constraint enforcement is preserved under INT4 quantization (4$\\times$ compression; Appendix~\\ref{app:quantization})."
}

    "newString": "OntoCoDec compiles IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints, validated across 3 datasets, 3+ models, and 2 tasks.\nThe central empirical finding is non-additive layer interaction: grammar alone achieves 3\\% DR reduction on SciERC, semantic alone 24\\%, but their composition yields 94\\% at 8B (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results})---an enabling dependency where grammar-enforced vocabulary membership creates the clean input the semantic layer requires.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale-dependent layer interaction.}\nThis dependency weakens at scale: at 70B, the semantic layer independently achieves 84\\% DR reduction (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}).\nWe hypothesize this reflects improved vocabulary calibration: larger models assign higher probability to ontology-valid tokens, reducing the semantic layer's dependence on grammar-enforced vocabulary membership (Appendix~\\ref{app:causal-tracing}).\nSupporting evidence includes the fallback frequency gap: under LoRA + S1, Qwen-8B triggers 0 grammar-first fallbacks on SciERC while Llama-8B triggers 5{,}882 (Table~\\ref{tab:ft-ablation}), yet both achieve DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\%, confirming that fallback frequency does not determine enforcement effectiveness.\nGrammar-constrained decoding is thus necessary but increasingly insufficient as models improve; constraint enforcement is preserved under INT4 quantization (4$\\times$ compression; Appendix~\\ref{app:quantization}).",
    "originalFile": "% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion\n\nOntoCoDec compiles IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints, validated across 3 datasets, 3+ models, and 2 tasks.\nBecause IE type constraints exceed context-free expressiveness (Proposition~1), the semantic layer bridges the gap grammar-only enforcement cannot address (3\\% vs.\\ 94\\% DR reduction at 8B), using constructive forward masking over a dynamic entity registry.\nThis scale-dependent interaction (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results})---likely reflecting improved vocabulary calibration at larger scale, where 70B models assign higher probability to ontology-valid tokens (Appendix~\\ref{app:causal-tracing})---suggests grammar-constrained decoding is necessary but increasingly insufficient as models improve; constraint enforcement is preserved under INT4 quantization (4$\\times$ compression; Appendix~\\ref{app:quantization}).\n\n\\paragraph{DR$\\leftrightarrow$CF trade-off.}\nGreedy S1 reduces DR to $\\leq$\\,0.21\\% but introduces CF (zero-shot $+$9.82\\,pp, fine-tuned $+$1.18\\,pp)---an architectural consequence of per-triple independent enforcement.\nAmong 248 zero-shot CF violations, 58.1\\% involve hallucinated entities (absent from gold) and only 7.3\\% are true cascade errors (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}); fine-tuned CF drops to $\\leq$1.4\\%.\nBeam $k{=}2$ reduces CF to 0.11\\% at 1.18$\\times$ latency (Pareto-optimal; recommended for zero-shot deployment); $k{=}4$ eliminates all violations at 8B (1 residual CF at 70B) at 1.39$\\times$ (\\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\nNet effect: total violations decrease ${\\sim}$31\\% in zero-shot and ${>}$90\\% under fine-tuning; DR is irrecoverable while CF is amenable to post-hoc resolution (TCR; Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}).\n\n\\paragraph{Complementarity and deployment.}\nGoLLIE and KnowCoder internalize schema knowledge via training but lack runtime guarantees; LoRA + S1 shows the two are complementary (DR 1--2\\%\\,$\\to$\\,$\\leq$0.21\\%; neither alone achieves both high R-F1 and near-zero DR).\nS2 (two-pass) exhibits scale-sensitive behavior: comparable to S1 at 8B but $-$8\\,pp R-F1 at 70B, consistent with CRANE's~\\cite{banerjee2025crane} finding that aggressive constraint enforcement can interfere with well-calibrated reasoning; we recommend S1 as the default configuration.\n\n\\paragraph{Per-triple vs.\\ global enforcement.}\nPer-triple enforcement cannot coordinate cross-triple type assignments---the source of CF (Level~3; \\S\\ref{sec:decidability}); global entity-level type consensus exceeds online per-token masking.\nBeam search and TCR approximate this; integrating consensus into decoding remains future work.\n",

  "content": "[Director 指令] [Patch] R26 审稿修复批次 — EMNLP 2026 DDL May 25, 紧急。\n\n以下是 R26 审稿报告的逐条修复指令。按优先级排序执行。引用不需要验证（时间紧）。\n\n---\n\n## 1. MF1 [Critical] Abstract 重构 — lead with total violation net effect\n\nFile: `artifacts/_project/paper/abstract.tex`, lines 9-12\n\n将当前 lines 9-12（从 \"Evaluated across\" 到 \"layer interaction.\"）替换为：\n\n```\nEvaluated across three 8B-class models and two relation extraction datasets (up to 96 relations), OntoCoDec composed with LoRA fine-tuning reduces total schema violations by ${>}$90\\% (DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\%; residual CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\% from per-triple enforcement, amenable to post-hoc resolution) at F1 40--46\\%.\nBeam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types at 1.39$\\times$ latency.\nIn zero-shot settings, total violations decrease ${\\sim}$31\\% (DR reductions 76--99\\% on SciERC, 42--90\\% on DocRED; CF up to 12\\%, decreasing with scale and fine-tuning).\n```\n\n关键变化：(a) 首句 lead with \">90% total reduction\"；(b) CF 定位为 \"residual\" + \"amenable to post-hoc resolution\"；(c) ZS 也 lead with total ~31%；(d) 删除 k=2 细节和尾句（正文有）以控制字数 ≤200。\n\n---\n\n## 2. SF6 [Major] C1 Mistral coverage overclaim\n\nFile: `artifacts/_project/paper/section1_introduction_draft.tex`, line 27\n\n将 C1 中的：\n`3 model families (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B) and 2 datasets (SciERC, DocRED) under both zero-shot and LoRA fine-tuned settings`\n\n替换为：\n`3 model families (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B) and 2 datasets (SciERC, DocRED) in zero-shot settings; Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B additionally under LoRA fine-tuning`\n\n---\n\n## 3. SF1 [Major] §7 \"3 datasets\" overclaim\n\nFile: `artifacts/_project/paper/section7_conclusion_compressed.tex`, line 3\n\n将：\n`validated across 3 datasets, 3+ models, and 2 tasks`\n\n替换为：\n`validated across 2 RE datasets (SciERC, DocRED) and 3 model families, with a preliminary event extraction pilot (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents})`\n\n---\n\n## 4. SF2 [Major] §7 vocabulary calibration speculative marker\n\nFile: `artifacts/_project/paper/section7_conclusion_compressed.tex`, line 7-8\n\n将：\n`We hypothesize this reflects improved vocabulary calibration: larger models assign higher probability to ontology-valid tokens, reducing the semantic layer's dependence on grammar-enforced vocabulary membership (Appendix~\\ref{app:causal-tracing}).`\n\n替换为：\n`We offer a speculative hypothesis: larger models exhibit improved vocabulary calibration, assigning higher probability to ontology-valid tokens and reducing the semantic layer's dependence on grammar-enforced vocabulary membership (Appendix~\\ref{app:causal-tracing}); confirming this causal mechanism requires cross-family verification beyond our current scope.`\n\n---\n\n## 5. SF4 [Warning] §5 Setup 添加 supervised SOTA 参照数字\n\nFile: `artifacts/_project/paper/section3_violation_landscape.tex`\n\n在 \"supervised span-based methods operating under different assumptions (gold entity spans, dedicated architectures) are not directly comparable.\" 之后，添加：\n\n`For reference, the supervised end-to-end SOTA on SciERC achieves 41.6\\% strict Rel-F1 (PL-Marker~\\cite{ye2022packed}) and 36.8\\% (PURE~\\cite{zhong2021frustratingly}); while evaluation protocols differ, our 40--46\\% R-F1 range under LoRA falls within a comparable band.`\n\n注意：R26 Reviewer 估计 supervised SOTA \"60%+ F1\" 是错误的——PL-Marker 在 SciERC end-to-end strict Rel+ F1 只有 41.6%。这个数字对我们有利。\n\n---\n\n## 6. SF5 [Warning] GoLLIE F1 参考数字\n\nFile: `artifacts/_project/paper/section6_related_work.tex`, line 13\n\n将：\n`GoLLIE achieves schema compliance through annotation-guideline-guided generation at training time; OntoCoDec provides orthogonal runtime enforcement`\n\n替换为：\n`GoLLIE achieves schema compliance through annotation-guideline-guided generation at training time (reporting 63.6--70.1\\% RE F1 on ACE05 in supervised settings; SciERC RE not evaluated); OntoCoDec provides orthogonal runtime enforcement`\n\n---\n\n## 7. W8 [Info→should-fix] §3 PL theory known-ness acknowledge\n\nFile: `artifacts/_project/paper/section3_method_draft.tex`\n\n在 line 23（\"Formally, domain/range constraints...\" 开头的句子之前），插入：\n\n`While cross-reference binding is known to exceed context-free expressiveness in programming language theory, its implications for IE-specific type constraints---where entity types are inferred from text rather than statically declared---have not been formally characterized.`\n\n---\n\n## 8. SF3 [Major] Appendix O position/name gap — 检查已有内容\n\nFile: `artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_pumping_lemma.tex`\n\n**注意**：Appendix O 末尾已有 \"Position indexing vs. name referencing\" 段落，内容覆盖了 R26-W4 的核心关切（PDA 无法随机访问 name→type 映射 + name-based 严格 subsume position-based）。\n\n请检查该段落是否充分。如果 Reviewer 认为不够，建议在该段落中添加一句明确承认 Case 1 pumping argument 的 gap：\n\n在 \"The core argument transfers directly\" 之前插入：\n`Specifically, Case~1 of the pumping argument (position shift) does not directly apply under name-based referencing, since pumping does not change which entity a name refers to. However, the non-context-free conclusion still holds via a stronger argument:`\n\n然后现有内容自然衔接。\n\n---\n\n## 9. Missing references — 添加 bib 条目\n\n在 .bib 文件中添加 DyGIE++：\n\n```bibtex\n@inproceedings{wadden2019entity,\n  title={Entity, Relation, and Event Extraction with Contextualized Span Representations},\n  author={Wadden, David and Wennberg, Ulme and Luan, Yi and Hajishirzi, Hannaneh},\n  booktitle={EMNLP},\n  year={2019}\n}\n```\n\n如果 Microsoft Guidance 在正文有引用也需要添加，否则跳过。\n\n---\n\n## 10. M1 [Info] F1 range 验证\n\n已验证：Table 2 (tab:composability) 数据范围 40.56-45.47，Abstract \"F1 40--46%\" 准确。无需修改。\n\n---\n\n## 11. M3 [Info] LoRA caveat\n\n已验证：§5 Setup (section3_violation_landscape.tex) 已有 \"LoRA hyperparameters... tuned on SciERC and transferred to DocRED without dataset-specific optimization\" 的 caveat。R26-M3 可能指实验叙述正文。如果觉得需要加强，在 §5.1 composability 段落开头加一句交叉引用即可：`(LoRA hyperparameters transferred from SciERC; \\S\\ref{sec:violation_landscape})`。低优先级。\n\n---\n\n执行完成后 compile 检查 ≤8 页 + ≤200 words abstract。不用验证引用。"
}

1	% Appendix: Full Proof of Proposition 1
2	% Level 2 (prefix-decidable with state) constraints are not context-free.
3	
4	\section{Full Proof of Proposition 1}
5	\label{app:pumping-lemma}
6	
7	We prove that domain/range type constraints for IE with unbounded entity sets cannot be expressed by any context-free grammar, using the pumping lemma for context-free languages.
8	
9	\begin{proof}
10	Define the language $L$ of valid IE outputs over ontology $\mathcal{O} = (\mathcal{E}, \mathcal{R}, \sigma)$ with $\mathcal{E} = \{\tau_A, \tau_B\}$, $\mathcal{R} = \{r\}$, and $\sigma(r) = \{(\tau_A, \tau_A)\}$ (i.e., $r$ requires both head and tail to be type $\tau_A$).
11	
12	An output string has the form:
13	\[
14	w = e_1\, e_2\, \cdots\, e_n\;\; \texttt{REL}(h{=}e_k,\, r,\, \tau^h)
15	\]
16	where each $e_i$ is an entity with type $\tau_{a_i} \in \{\tau_A, \tau_B\}$, and $\tau^h$ is the head type field of a relation triple referencing entity $e_k$ ($1 \leq k \leq n$).
17	
18	The type-validity constraint requires $\tau^h = \tau_{a_k}$ (the head type must match the referenced entity's type) \emph{and} $\tau^h \in \operatorname{dom}(r) = \{\tau_A\}$.
19	Combined: a string $w \in L$ if and only if $\tau_{a_k} = \tau_A$.
20	
21	Now consider strings of the specific form:
22	\[
23	w_n = \underbrace{\tau_B\; \tau_B\; \cdots\; \tau_B}_{n-1}\; \tau_A\;\; \texttt{REL}(h{=}e_n,\, r,\, \tau_A)
24	\]
25	where the first $n{-}1$ entities have type $\tau_B$ and only entity $e_n$ has type $\tau_A$.
26	The relation references $e_n$, so $\tau^h = \tau_A = \tau_{a_n}$, satisfying both the registry consistency and domain constraint.
27	Thus $w_n \in L$ for all $n$.
28	
29	Assume for contradiction that $L$ is context-free.
30	By the pumping lemma, there exists a pumping length $p$ such that any $w \in L$ with $|w| \geq p$ can be written as $w = uvxyz$ where:
31	\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
32	    \item $|vy| \geq 1$,
33	    \item $|vxy| \leq p$,
34	    \item $uv^i xy^i z \in L$ for all $i \geq 0$.
35	\end{enumerate}
36	
37	Choose $w = w_n$ with $n > p$.
38	The entity list portion $\tau_B^{n-1}\, \tau_A$ has length $n > p$, so the pumpable region $vxy$ (with $|vxy| \leq p$) must lie entirely within the entity list (it cannot span from the entity list to the relation reference, which is separated by $n - p > 0$ entity tokens from the start).
39	
40	\textbf{Case 1:} $v$ or $y$ contains entity tokens from the $\tau_B^{n-1}$ prefix.
41	Pumping up ($i = 2$): $uv^2 xy^2 z$ inserts additional $\tau_B$ entities, increasing $n$ to some $n' > n$.
42	But the relation reference still points to entity $e_n$ (at the original position), which is now at a different position in the longer entity list.
43	The entity at position $n$ in the pumped string is $\tau_B$ (since we inserted $\tau_B$ tokens before position $n$, shifting $\tau_A$ to position $n' > n$).
44	Thus $\tau^h = \tau_A \neq \tau_B = \tau_{a_n'}$, where $n'$ is the new position of entity $e_n$.
45	More precisely: the relation references a fixed entity name $e_k$, but pumping changes the entity-type binding at that name's position.
46	Since the pumped string assigns $\tau_B$ at position $k$ (which originally had $\tau_A$), the type-validity constraint $\tau^h = \tau_{a_k} \in \operatorname{dom}(r)$ is violated.
47	Hence $uv^2 xy^2 z \notin L$, contradicting condition (iii).
48	
49	\textbf{Case 2:} $v$ or $y$ contains the $\tau_A$ token at position $n$.
50	Pumping down ($i = 0$): $uxz$ removes the only $\tau_A$ entity.
51	The relation's head entity reference now points to a position that either does not exist or has type $\tau_B$.
52	In either case, $\tau^h = \tau_A \neq \tau_B$, violating type validity.
53	Hence $uxz \notin L$, contradicting condition (iii).
54	
55	In both cases, the pumping lemma is violated, so $L$ is not context-free.
56	The key insight is that type-validity requires a \emph{binding} between an entity's type (assigned at an earlier position) and the relation's type field (at a later position), with the binding target determined by the entity name.
57	Pumping disrupts this binding by changing which entity occupies a given position, while the relation reference remains fixed---a cross-position dependency that pushdown automata cannot track with their LIFO stack discipline.
58	
59	OntoCoDec's semantic layer addresses this with a runtime entity registry $\mathcal{D}$ (a finite map), operating as a deterministic stateful transducer that provides random-access type lookups, beyond the expressive power of context-free grammars.
60	\end{proof}
61	
62	\paragraph{Finite entity sets.}
63	For a finite entity set $\mathcal{E}$ with canonical identifiers, the valid language $L_{\text{valid}} = \bigcap_i L_i$ is theoretically regular (a finite intersection of regular languages).
64	However, the corresponding DFA requires $O(|\mathcal{T}|^{|\mathcal{E}|})$ states---for SciERC with 6 entity types and typical documents containing 20+ entities, this exceeds $10^{15}$ states, rendering explicit construction impractical.
65	Moreover, real IE operates over open-vocabulary text spans rather than closed identifier sets, making the general (non-regular) formulation the appropriate model.
66	Proposition~1 motivates the runtime state-tracking architecture for this general case.
67	
68	\paragraph{Position indexing vs.\ name referencing.}
69	The proof above uses position-based entity indexing ($e_k$ at position $k$) for expository clarity.
70	In OntoCoDec's actual JSON output, relation triples reference entities by name strings rather than positional indices.
71	The core argument transfers directly: validating type consistency requires a name$\to$type lookup over dynamically generated bindings, which is a random-access map operation.
72	A PDA's LIFO stack cannot perform random-access retrieval by key (it can only inspect the top element), so the non-context-free result holds regardless of whether entities are referenced by position or by name.
73	Name-based referencing strictly subsumes position-based indexing: any position-based constraint can be expressed via names (assign names $e_1, \ldots, e_n$ in order), but name-based constraints additionally permit cross-field coreference (the same entity name appearing in multiple triples must carry the same type), which position indexing cannot express.
74	Thus, the position-based formulation is a simplification that makes the pumping argument more transparent without weakening the conclusion.
75	
```

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Large language models for information extraction generate structurally valid but ontologically invalid triples---where entity types violate the ontology's domain/range constraints.
3	These violations persist across the capability spectrum and cannot be addressed by grammar-constrained decoding (which lacks cross-field type awareness) or post-hoc filtering (which discards valid predictions).
4	
5	We present \textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that compiles information extraction ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints.
6	A decidability analysis shows that domain/range constraints exceed context-free expressiveness, yielding a two-layer architecture: a grammar layer enforces structural validity and vocabulary membership; a semantic layer enforces type constraints at runtime through stateful masking over a dynamic entity registry.
7	
8	% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval
9	Evaluated across three 8B-class models and two relation extraction datasets (up to 96 relations), OntoCoDec composed with LoRA fine-tuning reduces domain/range violations to near-zero ($\leq$\,0.21\%) at F1 40--46\%, but introduces cross-field type inconsistencies ($\leq$1.4\%) from per-triple enforcement.
10	Beam search ($k{=}2$) reduces CF to 0.11\% at 1.18$\times$ latency; $k{=}4$ eliminates all violation types at 8B at 1.39$\times$.
11	In zero-shot settings, cross-field rates are higher (up to 12\%) but decrease with model calibration.
12	Zero-shot domain/range reductions span 76--99\% on SciERC and 42--90\% on DocRED; with scale- and schema-dependent layer interaction.
13	\end{abstract}
14	
```

```text
1	% §5 Setup (compressed from Violation Landscape)
2	% exp_ids: mvp_phase0_violation_rate, mvp_phase0_scierc_schema,
3	%          mvp_phase0_violation_rate_docred, mvp_phase0_docred_schema
4	
5	\label{sec:violation_landscape}
6	
7	We evaluate OntoCoDec in two deployment configurations: greedy S1 (DR\,$\leq$\,0.21\%, $+$10\% latency) and beam S1 (all violations\,$\to$\,0\%, 1.39$\times$ latency); zero-shot evaluation (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-zs}) serves as a diagnostic probe for the fine-tuned setting (R-F1 40--46\%).
8	
9	\paragraph{Setup.}
10	We evaluate on SciERC~\cite{luan2018scierc} (551 test sentences, 7 relations, $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.333$) and DocRED~\cite{yao2019docred} (998 development documents; test annotations are not publicly available; 96 relations, $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$).
11	RE type constraints are extracted from observed type pairs in training data (neither dataset provides a formal ontology); EE constraints on WikiEvents (\S\ref{sec:wikievents}) derive from the KAIROS ontology.
12	All constraints are schema-level, independent of evaluation splits.
13	Primary model: Qwen3-8B~\cite{qwen2025qwen3}, zero-shot greedy decoding; cross-model validation in Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-zs}, composability with fine-tuning in \S\ref{sec:composability}.
14	LoRA hyperparameters (rank, $\alpha$, learning rate) were tuned on SciERC and transferred to DocRED without dataset-specific optimization; given the substantial schema complexity difference (7 vs.\ 96 relations), this transfer may underestimate DocRED fine-tuning potential.
15	Our R-F1 range (40--46\% under LoRA) is consistent with recent LLM-based generative RE evaluations on these benchmarks, where document-level closed IE without gold entity spans typically achieves 35--50\%~\cite{wadhwa2023revisiting}; supervised span-based methods operating under different assumptions (gold entity spans, dedicated architectures) are not directly comparable.
16	DocRED development documents were used for evaluation only.
17	Four violation categories: \textbf{DR} (type pair $\notin \sigma(r)$), \textbf{RV} (relation $\notin \mathcal{R}$), \textbf{ET} (entity type $\notin \mathcal{E}$), \textbf{CF} (conflicting types across triples).
18	Baseline DR: 15.37\% (SciERC), 18.46\% (DocRED); schema-informed prompting reduces DR by 62\% but is per-relation unreliable (Appendix~\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).
19	
```

      "content": "% §5 Setup (compressed from Violation Landscape)\n% exp_ids: mvp_phase0_violation_rate, mvp_phase0_scierc_schema,\n%          mvp_phase0_violation_rate_docred, mvp_phase0_docred_schema\n\n\\label{sec:violation_landscape}\n\nWe evaluate OntoCoDec in two deployment configurations: greedy S1 (DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\%, $+$10\\% latency) and beam S1 (all violations\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%, 1.39$\\times$ latency); zero-shot evaluation (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}) serves as a diagnostic probe for the fine-tuned setting (R-F1 40--46\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Setup.}\nWe evaluate on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} (551 test sentences, 7 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$) and DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} (998 development documents; test annotations are not publicly available; 96 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$).\nRE type constraints are extracted from observed type pairs in training data (neither dataset provides a formal ontology); EE constraints on WikiEvents (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}) derive from the KAIROS ontology.\nAll constraints are schema-level, independent of evaluation splits.\nPrimary model: Qwen3-8B~\\cite{qwen2025qwen3}, zero-shot greedy decoding; cross-model validation in Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}, composability with fine-tuning in \\S\\ref{sec:composability}.\nLoRA hyperparameters (rank, $\\alpha$, learning rate) were tuned on SciERC and transferred to DocRED without dataset-specific optimization; given the substantial schema complexity difference (7 vs.\\ 96 relations), this transfer may underestimate DocRED fine-tuning potential.\nOur R-F1 range (40--46\\% under LoRA) is consistent with recent LLM-based generative RE evaluations on these benchmarks, where document-level closed IE without gold entity spans typically achieves 35--50\\%~\\cite{wadhwa2023revisiting}; supervised span-based methods operating under different assumptions (gold entity spans, dedicated architectures) are not directly comparable.\nDocRED development documents were used for evaluation only.\nFour violation categories: \\textbf{DR} (type pair $\\notin \\sigma(r)$), \\textbf{RV} (relation $\\notin \\mathcal{R}$), \\textbf{ET} (entity type $\\notin \\mathcal{E}$), \\textbf{CF} (conflicting types across triples).\nBaseline DR: 15.37\\% (SciERC), 18.46\\% (DocRED); schema-informed prompting reduces DR by 62\\% but is per-relation unreliable (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\n",

      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 19
    }

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01FaWjUiDqGTyKzi4s4eSasx`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	We provide a detailed survey in Appendix~\ref{app:related_work}; here we position OntoCoDec along three axes.
5	
6	\paragraph{Constrained decoding.}
7	XGrammar~\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, Outlines~\cite{willard2023outlines}, SynCode~\cite{ugare2024syncode}, SGLang~\cite{zheng2024sglang}, and related systems~\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql,park2024grammar,jsonschemabench2025} enforce format validity via CFG-based token masking but lack cross-field semantic awareness.
8	PICARD~\cite{scholak2021picard} and \citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} compile type systems into prefix automata; KG-Trie~\cite{luo2025kgtrie} applies trie-based KG constraints during decoding for knowledge-to-text generation.
9	OntoCoDec applies constructive forward masking via a dynamic entity registry, enforcing schema semantics within a single forward pass.
10	
11	\paragraph{Schema-aware IE.}
12	GoLLIE~\cite{sainz2024gollie}, KnowCoder~\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}, and others~\cite{guidex2025,josifoski2022genie,paolini2021tanl,cabot2021rebel,wang2023instructuie,qi2024adelie,fei2022lasuie,lu2022uie,zhou2024universalner} encode ontology structure via training-time internalization without runtime enforcement~\cite{wadhwa2023revisiting}.
13	GoLLIE achieves schema compliance through annotation-guideline-guided generation at training time; OntoCoDec provides orthogonal runtime enforcement, composable with any generative IE system including GoLLIE itself (Appendix~\ref{app:gollie-comparison}).
14	
15	\paragraph{Constraint enforcement in IE.}
16	\citet{roth_yih_2004} and \citet{riedel2010modeling} formulated hard type constraints via ILP; post-hoc validation~\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025} detects violations after generation but discards up to 74\% of predictions.
17	EnriCo~\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} uses ASP; IterGen~\cite{ugare2025itergen} backtracks on violations; \citet{vukovic2024dialogue} combines ontology constraints with constrained chain-of-thought for dialogue RE---OntoCoDec uses forward-only masking at $O(|V|)$ per step without iterative refinement.
18	Grammar-only enforcement (GCD~\cite{geng2023gcd}, BoostCD~\cite{boostcd2025}) achieves only 3\% DR reduction (\S\ref{sec:constrained-baselines}).
19	
```

</details>

    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section7_conclusion_compressed.tex",
    "oldString": "We hypothesize this reflects improved vocabulary calibration: larger models assign higher probability to ontology-valid tokens, reducing the semantic layer's dependence on grammar-enforced vocabulary membership (Appendix~\\ref{app:causal-tracing}).",
    "newString": "We offer a speculative hypothesis: larger models exhibit improved vocabulary calibration, assigning higher probability to ontology-valid tokens and reducing the semantic layer's dependence on grammar-enforced vocabulary membership (Appendix~\\ref{app:causal-tracing}); confirming this causal mechanism requires cross-family verification beyond our current scope.",
    "originalFile": "% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion\n\nOntoCoDec compiles IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints, validated across 2 RE datasets (SciERC, DocRED) and 3 model families, with a preliminary event extraction pilot (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}).\nThe central empirical finding is non-additive layer interaction: grammar alone achieves 3\\% DR reduction on SciERC, semantic alone 24\\%, but their composition yields 94\\% at 8B (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results})---an enabling dependency where grammar-enforced vocabulary membership creates the clean input the semantic layer requires.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale-dependent layer interaction.}\nThis dependency weakens at scale: at 70B, the semantic layer independently achieves 84\\% DR reduction (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}).\nWe hypothesize this reflects improved vocabulary calibration: larger models assign higher probability to ontology-valid tokens, reducing the semantic layer's dependence on grammar-enforced vocabulary membership (Appendix~\\ref{app:causal-tracing}).\nSupporting evidence includes the fallback frequency gap: under LoRA + S1, Qwen-8B triggers 0 grammar-first fallbacks on SciERC while Llama-8B triggers 5{,}882 (Table~\\ref{tab:ft-ablation}), yet both achieve DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\%, confirming that fallback frequency does not determine enforcement effectiveness.\nGrammar-constrained decoding is thus necessary but increasingly insufficient as models improve; constraint enforcement is preserved under INT4 quantization (4$\\times$ compression; Appendix~\\ref{app:quantization}).\n\n\\paragraph{DR$\\leftrightarrow$CF trade-off.}\nGreedy S1 reduces DR to $\\leq$\\,0.21\\% but introduces CF (zero-shot $+$9.82\\,pp, fine-tuned $+$1.18\\,pp)---an architectural consequence of per-triple independent enforcement.\nAmong 248 zero-shot CF violations, 58.1\\% involve hallucinated entities (absent from gold) and only 7.3\\% are true cascade errors (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}); fine-tuned CF drops to $\\leq$1.4\\%.\nBeam $k{=}2$ reduces CF to 0.11\\% at 1.18$\\times$ latency (Pareto-optimal; recommended for zero-shot deployment); $k{=}4$ eliminates all violations at 8B (1 residual CF at 70B) at 1.39$\\times$ (\\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\nNet effect: total violations decrease ${\\sim}$31\\% in zero-shot and ${>}$90\\% under fine-tuning; DR is irrecoverable while CF is amenable to post-hoc resolution (TCR; Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}).\n\n\\paragraph{Complementarity and deployment.}\nGoLLIE and KnowCoder internalize schema knowledge via training but lack runtime guarantees; LoRA + S1 shows the two are complementary (DR 1--2\\%\\,$\\to$\\,$\\leq$0.21\\%; neither alone achieves both high R-F1 and near-zero DR).\nS2 (two-pass) exhibits scale-sensitive behavior: comparable to S1 at 8B but $-$8\\,pp R-F1 at 70B, consistent with CRANE's~\\cite{banerjee2025crane} finding that aggressive constraint enforcement can interfere with well-calibrated reasoning; we recommend S1 as the default configuration.\n\n\\paragraph{Per-triple vs.\\ global enforcement.}\nPer-triple enforcement cannot coordinate cross-triple type assignments---the source of CF (Level~3; \\S\\ref{sec:decidability}); global entity-level type consensus exceeds online per-token masking.\nBeam search and TCR approximate this; integrating consensus into decoding remains future work.\n",

    "oldString": "Evaluated across three 8B-class models and two relation extraction datasets (up to 96 relations), OntoCoDec composed with LoRA fine-tuning reduces domain/range violations to near-zero ($\\leq$\\,0.21\\%) at F1 40--46\\%, but introduces cross-field type inconsistencies ($\\leq$1.4\\%) from per-triple enforcement.\nBeam search ($k{=}2$) reduces CF to 0.11\\% at 1.18$\\times$ latency; $k{=}4$ eliminates all violation types at 8B at 1.39$\\times$.\nIn zero-shot settings, cross-field rates are higher (up to 12\\%) but decrease with model calibration.\nZero-shot domain/range reductions span 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED; with scale- and schema-dependent layer interaction.",
    "newString": "Evaluated across three 8B-class models and two relation extraction datasets (up to 96 relations), OntoCoDec composed with LoRA fine-tuning reduces total schema violations by ${>}$90\\% (DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\%; residual CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\% from per-triple enforcement, amenable to post-hoc resolution) at F1 40--46\\%.\nBeam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types at 1.39$\\times$ latency.\nIn zero-shot settings, total violations decrease ${\\sim}$31\\% (DR reductions 76--99\\% on SciERC, 42--90\\% on DocRED; CF up to 12\\%, decreasing with scale and fine-tuning).",
    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nLarge language models for information extraction generate structurally valid but ontologically invalid triples---where entity types violate the ontology's domain/range constraints.\nThese violations persist across the capability spectrum and cannot be addressed by grammar-constrained decoding (which lacks cross-field type awareness) or post-hoc filtering (which discards valid predictions).\n\nWe present \\textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that compiles information extraction ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints.\nA decidability analysis shows that domain/range constraints exceed context-free expressiveness, yielding a two-layer architecture: a grammar layer enforces structural validity and vocabulary membership; a semantic layer enforces type constraints at runtime through stateful masking over a dynamic entity registry.\n\n% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\nEvaluated across three 8B-class models and two relation extraction datasets (up to 96 relations), OntoCoDec composed with LoRA fine-tuning reduces domain/range violations to near-zero ($\\leq$\\,0.21\\%) at F1 40--46\\%, but introduces cross-field type inconsistencies ($\\leq$1.4\\%) from per-triple enforcement.\nBeam search ($k{=}2$) reduces CF to 0.11\\% at 1.18$\\times$ latency; $k{=}4$ eliminates all violation types at 8B at 1.39$\\times$.\nIn zero-shot settings, cross-field rates are higher (up to 12\\%) but decrease with model calibration.\nZero-shot domain/range reductions span 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED; with scale- and schema-dependent layer interaction.\n\\end{abstract}\n",

    "oldString": "3 model families (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B) and 2 datasets (SciERC, DocRED) under both zero-shot and LoRA fine-tuned settings",
    "newString": "3 model families (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B) and 2 datasets (SciERC, DocRED) in zero-shot settings; Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B additionally under LoRA fine-tuning",
    "originalFile": "% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity necessarily carries a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.\nWe call such outputs \\emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.\nViolations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\\% of triples on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\\% on DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 0.5--2\\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates follow an observed pattern consistent with schema restrictiveness (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.\nAt competitive quality (R-F1 40--46\\%), even 1--2\\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption, making reliable constraint enforcement necessary.\n\nExisting approaches leave a fundamental gap.\nSchema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \\textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \\emph{increase} by 5.8\\,pp).\nGrammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} ensures syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.\nPost-hoc filtering discards up to 74\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\nTraining-based approaches~\\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} internalize schema knowledge but provide only soft guidance without runtime enforcement.\n\nWe propose \\textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.\nA decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \\emph{grammar layer} compiles ontology constraints into context-free grammars (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \\emph{semantic layer} interprets type constraints at runtime via dynamic logit masking over an entity registry, with a scale-dependent layer interaction: at 8B, the semantic layer achieves only 24\\% independent DR reduction (near-complete when combined), while at 70B it reaches 84\\% independently.\nOntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and is applicable to autoregressive LMs with logits access; we validate on three English datasets (up to 96 relation types), three 8B-class models across three tokenizer families, with 70B-class validation (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}).\nLoRA + greedy S1 reduces DR to near-zero: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\% at R-F1 40--46\\% ($\\pm$0.3\\,pp, $+$10\\% latency); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types at 8B on both RE datasets under fine-tuning (1.39$\\times$ latency).\nDR enforcement converts ontologically invalid violations to individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\% fine-tuned); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED, with total violations decreasing $\\sim$31\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\n\nOntoCoDec is a constraint enforcement method, orthogonal and complementary to training-based schema-aware approaches such as GoLLIE~\\cite{sainz2024gollie} and KnowCoder~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}; it can be composed with any generative IE system to improve schema compliance without retraining.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]\n    \\item Systematic ablation across 3 model families (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B) and 2 datasets (SciERC, DocRED) under both zero-shot and LoRA fine-tuned settings, demonstrating DR reduction to $\\leq$0.21\\% with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.3\\,pp; DR enforcement introduces CF as a correctable side effect (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}).\n    \\item A two-layer constrained decoding framework grounded in the observation that domain/range type constraints require stateful enforcement beyond context-free grammars---grammar-only approaches face state spaces of $6^{17}$--$6^{19}$ for typical IE schemas (Proposition~1; \\S\\ref{sec:decidability}). Constraints compile automatically from IE ontologies, validated on schemas with up to 96 relation types.\n    \\item Preliminary single-family scale exploration on Llama-3.1-70B (AWQ INT4), revealing a scale-dependent layer interaction: the semantic layer alone achieves 84\\% DR reduction at 70B vs.\\ 24\\% at 8B (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}). At 8B, beam $k{=}2$ is Pareto-optimal (R-F1 44.60, CF 0.11\\%, 1.18$\\times$ greedy S1 latency); $k{=}4$ achieves DR${=}$0\\% with near-zero CF at 1.39$\\times$.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {

        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 24,

At 8B, beam $k{=}2$ is Pareto-optimal (R-F1 44.60, CF 0.11\\%, 1.18$\\times$ greedy S1 latency); $k{=}4$ achieves DR${=}$0\\% with near-zero CF at 1.39$\\times$.",

    "originalFile": "% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion\n\nOntoCoDec compiles IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints, validated across 3 datasets, 3+ models, and 2 tasks.\nThe central empirical finding is non-additive layer interaction: grammar alone achieves 3\\% DR reduction on SciERC, semantic alone 24\\%, but their composition yields 94\\% at 8B (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results})---an enabling dependency where grammar-enforced vocabulary membership creates the clean input the semantic layer requires.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale-dependent layer interaction.}\nThis dependency weakens at scale: at 70B, the semantic layer independently achieves 84\\% DR reduction (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}).\nWe hypothesize this reflects improved vocabulary calibration: larger models assign higher probability to ontology-valid tokens, reducing the semantic layer's dependence on grammar-enforced vocabulary membership (Appendix~\\ref{app:causal-tracing}).\nSupporting evidence includes the fallback frequency gap: under LoRA + S1, Qwen-8B triggers 0 grammar-first fallbacks on SciERC while Llama-8B triggers 5{,}882 (Table~\\ref{tab:ft-ablation}), yet both achieve DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\%, confirming that fallback frequency does not determine enforcement effectiveness.\nGrammar-constrained decoding is thus necessary but increasingly insufficient as models improve; constraint enforcement is preserved under INT4 quantization (4$\\times$ compression; Appendix~\\ref{app:quantization}).\n\n\\paragraph{DR$\\leftrightarrow$CF trade-off.}\nGreedy S1 reduces DR to $\\leq$\\,0.21\\% but introduces CF (zero-shot $+$9.82\\,pp, fine-tuned $+$1.18\\,pp)---an architectural consequence of per-triple independent enforcement.\nAmong 248 zero-shot CF violations, 58.1\\% involve hallucinated entities (absent from gold) and only 7.3\\% are true cascade errors (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}); fine-tuned CF drops to $\\leq$1.4\\%.\nBeam $k{=}2$ reduces CF to 0.11\\% at 1.18$\\times$ latency (Pareto-optimal; recommended for zero-shot deployment); $k{=}4$ eliminates all violations at 8B (1 residual CF at 70B) at 1.39$\\times$ (\\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\nNet effect: total violations decrease ${\\sim}$31\\% in zero-shot and ${>}$90\\% under fine-tuning; DR is irrecoverable while CF is amenable to post-hoc resolution (TCR; Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}).\n\n\\paragraph{Complementarity and deployment.}\nGoLLIE and KnowCoder internalize schema knowledge via training but lack runtime guarantees; LoRA + S1 shows the two are complementary (DR 1--2\\%\\,$\\to$\\,$\\leq$0.21\\%; neither alone achieves both high R-F1 and near-zero DR).\nS2 (two-pass) exhibits scale-sensitive behavior: comparable to S1 at 8B but $-$8\\,pp R-F1 at 70B, consistent with CRANE's~\\cite{banerjee2025crane} finding that aggressive constraint enforcement can interfere with well-calibrated reasoning; we recommend S1 as the default configuration.\n\n\\paragraph{Per-triple vs.\\ global enforcement.}\nPer-triple enforcement cannot coordinate cross-triple type assignments---the source of CF (Level~3; \\S\\ref{sec:decidability}); global entity-level type consensus exceeds online per-token masking.\nBeam search and TCR approximate this; integrating consensus into decoding remains future work.\n",

zero-shot evaluation (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}) serves as a diagnostic probe for the fine-tuned setting (R-F1 40--46\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Setup.}\nWe evaluate on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} (551 test sentences, 7 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$) and DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} (998 development documents; test annotations are not publicly available; 96 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$).\nRE type constraints are extracted from observed type pairs in training data (neither dataset provides a formal ontology); EE constraints on WikiEvents (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}) derive from the KAIROS ontology.\nAll constraints are schema-level, independent of evaluation splits.\nPrimary model: Qwen3-8B~\\cite{qwen2025qwen3}, zero-shot greedy decoding; cross-model validation in Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}, composability with fine-tuning in \\S\\ref{sec:composability}.\nLoRA hyperparameters (rank, $\\alpha$, learning rate) were tuned on SciERC and transferred to DocRED without dataset-specific optimization; given the substantial schema complexity difference (7 vs.\\ 96 relations), this transfer may underestimate DocRED fine-tuning potential.\nOur R-F1 range (40--46\\% under LoRA) is consistent with recent LLM-based generative RE evaluations on these benchmarks, where document-level closed IE without gold entity spans typically achieves 35--50\\%~\\cite{wadhwa2023revisiting}; supervised span-based methods operating under different assumptions (gold entity spans, dedicated architectures) are not directly comparable.\nDocRED development documents were used for evaluation only.\nFour violation categories: \\textbf{DR} (type pair $\\notin \\sigma(r)$), \\textbf{RV} (relation $\\notin \\mathcal{R}$), \\textbf{ET} (entity type $\\notin \\mathcal{E}$), \\textbf{CF} (conflicting types across triples).\nBaseline DR: 15.37\\% (SciERC), 18.46\\% (DocRED);     "newString": "Specifically, Case~1 of the pumping argument (position shift) does not directly apply under name-based referencing, since pumping does not change which entity a name refers to. However, the non-context-free conclusion still holds via a stronger argument:\nvalidating type consistency requires a name$\\to$type lookup",
    "originalFile": "% Appendix: Full Proof of Proposition 1\n% Level 2 (prefix-decidable with state) constraints are not context-free.\n\n\\section{Full Proof of Proposition 1}\n\\label{app:pumping-lemma}\n\nWe prove that domain/range type constraints for IE with unbounded entity sets cannot be expressed by any context-free grammar, using the pumping lemma for context-free languages.\n\n\\begin{proof}\nDefine the language $L$ of valid IE outputs over ontology $\\mathcal{O} = (\\mathcal{E}, \\mathcal{R}, \\sigma)$ with $\\mathcal{E} = \\{\\tau_A, \\tau_B\\}$, $\\mathcal{R} = \\{r\\}$, and $\\sigma(r) = \\{(\\tau_A, \\tau_A)\\}$ (i.e., $r$ requires both head and tail to be type $\\tau_A$).\n\nAn output string has the form:\n\\[\nw = e_1\\, e_2\\, \\cdots\\, e_n\\;\\; \\texttt{REL}(h{=}e_k,\\, r,\\, \\tau^h)\n\\]\nwhere each $e_i$ is an entity with type $\\tau_{a_i} \\in \\{\\tau_A, \\tau_B\\}$, and $\\tau^h$ is the head type field of a relation triple referencing entity $e_k$ ($1 \\leq k \\leq n$).\n\nThe type-validity constraint requires $\\tau^h = \\tau_{a_k}$ (the head type must match the referenced entity's type) \\emph{and} $\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r) = \\{\\tau_A\\}$.\nCombined: a string $w \\in L$ if and only if $\\tau_{a_k} = \\tau_A$.\n\nNow consider strings of the specific form:\n\\[\nw_n = \\underbrace{\\tau_B\\; \\tau_B\\; \\cdots\\; \\tau_B}_{n-1}\\; \\tau_A\\;\\; \\texttt{REL}(h{=}e_n,\\, r,\\, \\tau_A)\n\\]\nwhere the first $n{-}1$ entities have type $\\tau_B$ and only entity $e_n$ has type $\\tau_A$.\nThe relation references $e_n$, so $\\tau^h = \\tau_A = \\tau_{a_n}$, satisfying both the registry consistency and domain constraint.\nThus $w_n \\in L$ for all $n$.\n\nAssume for contradiction that $L$ is context-free.\nBy the pumping lemma, there exists a pumping length $p$ such that any $w \\in L$ with $|w| \\geq p$ can be written as $w = uvxyz$ where:\n\\begin{enumerate}[label=(\\roman*)]\n    \\item $|vy| \\geq 1$,\n    \\item $|vxy| \\leq p$,\n    \\item $uv^i xy^i z \\in L$ for all $i \\geq 0$.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nChoose $w = w_n$ with $n > p$.\nThe entity list portion $\\tau_B^{n-1}\\, \\tau_A$ has length $n > p$, so the pumpable region $vxy$ (with $|vxy| \\leq p$) must lie entirely within the entity list (it cannot span from the entity list to the relation reference, which is separated by $n - p > 0$ entity tokens from the start).\n\n\\textbf{Case 1:} $v$ or $y$ contains entity tokens from the $\\tau_B^{n-1}$ prefix.\nPumping up ($i = 2$): $uv^2 xy^2 z$ inserts additional $\\tau_B$ entities, increasing $n$ to some $n' > n$.\nBut the relation reference still points to entity $e_n$ (at the original position), which is now at a different position in the longer entity list.\nThe entity at position $n$ in the pumped string is $\\tau_B$ (since we inserted $\\tau_B$ tokens before position $n$, shifting $\\tau_A$ to position $n' > n$).\nThus $\\tau^h = \\tau_A \\neq \\tau_B = \\tau_{a_n'}$, where $n'$ is the new position of entity $e_n$.\nMore precisely: the relation references a fixed entity name $e_k$, but pumping changes the entity-type binding at that name's position.\nSince the pumped string assigns $\\tau_B$ at position $k$ (which originally had $\\tau_A$), the type-validity constraint $\\tau^h = \\tau_{a_k} \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r)$ is violated.\nHence $uv^2 xy^2 z \\notin L$, contradicting condition (iii).\n\n\\textbf{Case 2:} $v$ or $y$ contains the $\\tau_A$ token at position $n$.\nPumping down ($i = 0$): $uxz$ removes the only $\\tau_A$ entity.\nThe relation's head entity reference now points to a position that either does not exist or has type $\\tau_B$.\nIn either case, $\\tau^h = \\tau_A \\neq \\tau_B$, violating type validity.\nHence $uxz \\notin L$, contradicting condition (iii).\n\nIn both cases, the pumping lemma is violated, so $L$ is not context-free.\nThe key insight is that type-validity requires a \\emph{binding} between an entity's type (assigned at an earlier position) and the relation's type field (at a later position), with the binding target determined by the entity name.\nPumping disrupts this binding by changing which entity occupies a given position, while the relation reference remains fixed---a cross-position dependency that pushdown automata cannot track with their LIFO stack discipline.\n\nOntoCoDec's semantic layer addresses this with a runtime entity registry $\\mathcal{D}$ (a finite map), operating as a deterministic stateful transducer that provides random-access type lookups, beyond the expressive power of context-free grammars.\n\\end{proof}\n\n\\paragraph{Finite entity sets.}\nFor a finite entity set $\\mathcal{E}$ with canonical identifiers, the valid language $L_{\\text{valid}} = \\bigcap_i L_i$ is theoretically regular (a finite intersection of regular languages).\nHowever, the corresponding DFA requires $O(|\\mathcal{T}|^{|\\mathcal{E}|})$ states---for SciERC with 6 entity types and typical documents containing 20+ entities, this exceeds $10^{15}$ states, rendering explicit construction impractical.\nMoreover, real IE operates over open-vocabulary text spans rather than closed identifier sets, making the general (non-regular) formulation the appropriate model.\nProposition~1 motivates the runtime state-tracking architecture for this general case.\n\n\\paragraph{Position indexing vs.\\ name referencing.}\nThe proof above uses position-based entity indexing ($e_k$ at position $k$) for expository clarity.\nIn OntoCoDec's actual JSON output, relation triples reference entities by name strings rather than positional indices.\nThe core argument transfers directly: validating type consistency requires a name$\\to$type lookup over dynamically generated bindings, which is a random-access map operation.\nA PDA's LIFO stack cannot perform random-access retrieval by key (it can only inspect the top element), so the non-context-free result holds regardless of whether entities are referenced by position or by name.\nName-based referencing strictly subsumes position-based indexing: any position-based constraint can be expressed via names (assign names $e_1, \\ldots, e_n$ in order), but name-based constraints additionally permit cross-field coreference (the same entity name appearing in multiple triples must carry the same type), which position indexing cannot express.\nThus, the position-based formulation is a simplification that makes the pumping argument more transparent without weakening the conclusion.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\nWe provide a detailed survey in Appendix~\\ref{app:related_work}; here we position OntoCoDec along three axes.\n\n\\paragraph{Constrained decoding.}\nXGrammar~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, Outlines~\\cite{willard2023outlines}, SynCode~\\cite{ugare2024syncode}, SGLang~\\cite{zheng2024sglang}, and related systems~\\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql,park2024grammar,jsonschemabench2025} enforce format validity via CFG-based token masking but lack cross-field semantic awareness.\nPICARD~\\cite{scholak2021picard} and \\citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} compile type systems into prefix automata; KG-Trie~\\cite{luo2025kgtrie} applies trie-based KG constraints during decoding for knowledge-to-text generation.\nOntoCoDec applies constructive forward masking via a dynamic entity registry, enforcing schema semantics within a single forward pass.\n\n\\paragraph{Schema-aware IE.}\nGoLLIE~\\cite{sainz2024gollie}, KnowCoder~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}, and others~\\cite{guidex2025,josifoski2022genie,paolini2021tanl,cabot2021rebel,wang2023instructuie,qi2024adelie,fei2022lasuie,lu2022uie,zhou2024universalner} encode ontology structure via training-time internalization without runtime enforcement~\\cite{wadhwa2023revisiting}.\nGoLLIE achieves schema compliance through annotation-guideline-guided generation at training time; OntoCoDec provides orthogonal runtime enforcement, composable with any generative IE system including GoLLIE itself (Appendix~\\ref{app:gollie-comparison}).\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint enforcement in IE.}\n\\citet{roth_yih_2004} and \\citet{riedel2010modeling} formulated hard type constraints via ILP; post-hoc validation~\\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025} detects violations after generation but discards up to 74\\% of predictions.\nEnriCo~\\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} uses ASP; IterGen~\\cite{ugare2025itergen} backtracks on violations; \\citet{vukovic2024dialogue} combines ontology constraints with constrained chain-of-thought for dialogue RE---OntoCoDec uses forward-only masking at $O(|V|)$ per step without iterative refinement.\nGrammar-only enforcement (GCD~\\cite{geng2023gcd}, BoostCD~\\cite{boostcd2025}) achieves only 3\\% DR reduction (\\S\\ref{sec:constrained-baselines}).\n",

    "oldString": "Formally, domain/range constraints for unbounded entity sets are not context-free (\\textbf{Proposition~1}; proof via pumping argument in Appendix~\\ref{app:pumping-lemma})",
    "newString": "While cross-reference binding is known to exceed context-free expressiveness in programming language theory, its implications for IE-specific type constraints---where entity types are inferred from text rather than statically declared---have not been formally characterized.\nFormally, domain/range constraints for unbounded entity sets are not context-free (\\textbf{Proposition~1}; proof via pumping argument in Appendix~\\ref{app:pumping-lemma})",
    "originalFile": "% Section 3 — Method (Draft)\n% OntoCoDec: Type-Valid Information Extraction\n% via Compiled Ontology Constraints\n\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\input{fig1_framework}\n\nWe present OntoCoDec, a framework that translates IE ontologies into token-level constraints enforced during autoregressive generation.\nThe design is guided by a decidability analysis of IE type constraints.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint decidability taxonomy.}\n\\label{sec:decidability}\nWe classify IE type constraints into three levels: (1)~\\emph{context-free}: vocabulary membership and JSON structure; (2)~\\emph{prefix-decidable with state}: domain/range type validity, determined at each type-bearing position given the decoded relation type and entity registry; (3)~\\emph{non-local}: cross-entity type consistency, which depends on the full output.\nUnlike code generation where types are statically declared, IE constraints fall into level~2: entity types are inferred from text, the registry is built dynamically, and constraint targets depend on the co-occurring relation.\nOntoCoDec enforces levels 1--2 (grammar and semantic layers, respectively); level~3 is addressed by beam search or post-hoc resolution (Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}).\n\n\\smallskip\n\\noindent\nExisting grammar-constrained decoding frameworks (e.g., XGrammar) enforce level~1 constraints---structural validity and vocabulary membership---but IE domain/range rules require cross-position entity-type state that context-free grammars cannot express.\nEven when the entity set is finite, encoding type constraints into a DFA requires $O(|\\mathcal{E}|^{|\\text{entities}|})$ states: for typical SciERC documents (median 17 entities, 6 types), $6^{17} \\approx 1.6{\\times}10^{13}$ states; for DocRED (median 19 entities, 6 types), $6^{19} \\approx 6.1{\\times}10^{14}$---well beyond practical construction for online decoding.\nFormally, domain/range constraints for unbounded entity sets are not context-free (\\textbf{Proposition~1}; proof via pumping argument in Appendix~\\ref{app:pumping-lemma}): a PDA cannot retrieve the type of entity $e_k$ without destroying stack entries above it.\nOntoCoDec's semantic layer addresses this with a runtime entity registry $\\mathcal{D}$ (a finite map enabling random-access type lookups), avoiding both the theoretical impossibility (unbounded) and the engineering intractability (finite but exponential).\n\n\\noindent\\textbf{Empirical Observation} (scale-dependent enabling dependency)\\textbf{.} The grammar layer alone cannot enforce domain/range constraints (Proposition~1), while the semantic layer alone achieves only 24\\% DR reduction at 8B without grammar-enforced vocabulary membership (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}, Table~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}).\nThe strength of this dependency is scale-dependent: at 70B, the semantic layer independently achieves 84\\% DR reduction (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}), whereas 8B models show only 24\\% independent reduction.\n\\smallskip\n\nWe describe the problem formulation (\\S\\ref{sec:formulation}), each layer, and two decoding strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:strategies}); formal analysis of constraint composition is in Section~\\ref{sec:composition}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:formulation}\n\n\\paragraph{IE as structured generation.}\nGiven an input text $x$, a language model generates a structured JSON output $y$:\n%\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:output-schema}\n\\resizebox{.88\\columnwidth}{!}{$\\displaystyle\n  y = \\bigl\\{\\texttt{entities}: [\\{n_i, \\tau_i\\}],\\; \\texttt{relations}: [\\{h_j, t_j, r_j, \\tau^h_j, \\tau^t_j\\}]\\bigr\\}\n$}\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere each entity has a name $n_i$ and type $\\tau_i \\in \\mathcal{E}$, and each relation specifies head $h_j$, tail $t_j$, relation type $r_j \\in \\mathcal{R}$, and head/tail entity types $\\tau^h_j, \\tau^t_j$.\nThe output JSON Schema (\\S\\ref{sec:grammar}) enforces a fixed field ordering (head $\\to$ tail $\\to$ relation $\\to$ head\\_type $\\to$ tail\\_type) via deterministic grammar compilation, ensuring the relation type $r_j$ is known before entity type fields and enabling sequential domain/range lookups (\\S\\ref{sec:dynamic-masking}).\nThe grammar structurally enforces entity-before-relation ordering (maintained in all documents across all configurations), ensuring the entity registry (\\S\\ref{sec:entity-registry}) is populated before relation-level type constraints are evaluated; unregistered entities trigger domain-only filtering.\n\n\\paragraph{Ontology compliance.}\nUsing the ontology $\\mathcal{O} = (\\mathcal{E}, \\mathcal{R}, \\sigma)$ defined in \\S\\ref{sec:background}, we write $\\operatorname{dom}(r) = \\{\\tau^h : \\exists \\tau^t,\\, (\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)\\}$ for domain-valid head types and $\\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h) = \\{\\tau^t : (\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)\\}$ for the conditional range given head type $\\tau^h$.\nOur enforcement target is \\emph{pair-based} type validity: $(\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)$ for every triple.\nThe decoder enforces this sequentially: $\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r)$ at head position, then $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h)$ at tail position, achieving best-effort pair-based enforcement (pair validity ensured when no fallback is triggered; domain-only filtering otherwise) strictly stronger than independent domain/range checks (which over-permit 33--37\\% spurious pairs on SciERC/DocRED; \\S\\ref{sec:rectangularity}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Grammar Layer}\n\\label{sec:grammar}\n\nA JSON Schema encoding Eq.~\\eqref{eq:output-schema} with \\texttt{enum} constraints ($\\mathcal{E}$, $\\mathcal{R}$) is compiled into a pushdown automaton via XGrammar~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar}.\nAt step $t$, grammar-valid tokens $G(t) \\subseteq V$ pass; all others receive logits $-\\infty$, enforcing structural validity, vocabulary membership ($r_j \\in \\mathcal{R}$, $\\tau_i \\in \\mathcal{E}$), but not domain/range constraints (which depend on entity types within each triple).\n\n\\subsection{Semantic Layer}\n\\label{sec:semantic}\n\nThe semantic layer enforces domain/range constraints through a stateful logits processor maintaining an entity registry during generation.\n\\emph{Running example}: for \\textsc{Used-for} ($\\operatorname{dom}{=}\\{\\textrm{Method}\\}$, $\\operatorname{range}{=}\\{\\textrm{Task}\\}$), entity ``ResNet'' (Method) passes the head-type check while Material is blocked; ``image classification'' (Task) passes the tail-type check.\n\n\\paragraph{Entity registry.}\n\\label{sec:entity-registry}\nThe semantic layer builds a registry $\\mathcal{D}: \\text{name} \\to \\text{type}$ incrementally during entity generation: each completed entry $\\{n_i, \\tau_i\\}$ adds $(n_i, \\tau_i)$ to $\\mathcal{D}$, which is consulted during relation generation for type lookups.\nThe registry uses \\emph{first-seen} semantics: when an entity name appears in multiple triples, the type assigned at its first occurrence governs all subsequent constraint checks, making type enforcement sensitive to generation order.\nAt each step $t$, the processor pattern-matches the decoded prefix to identify the current field position; semantic masks activate only at type-bearing positions (\\textsc{HeadType}, \\textsc{TailType}).\n\\label{sec:position-detection}\n\n\\paragraph{Dynamic masking.}\n\\label{sec:dynamic-masking}\nAt type-bearing positions, the processor computes a semantic mask $S(t) \\subseteq V$.\nAt a \\textsc{HeadType} position, if $h_j \\in \\mathcal{D}$ and $\\mathcal{D}[h_j] \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r_j)$, the mask allows only the registered type; otherwise it falls back to all domain-valid types $\\operatorname{dom}(r_j)$.\nAt a \\textsc{TailType} position, the mask restricts to $\\operatorname{range}(r_j, \\tau^h_j)$ conditioned on the already-determined head type, further narrowing to the registered type when $t_j \\in \\mathcal{D}$.\nThis sequential check achieves best-effort pair-based enforcement (\\S\\ref{sec:formulation}), strictly tighter than independent domain/range checking; ``best-effort'' reflects the grammar-first fallback (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}), which prioritizes JSON integrity over type validity when constraints conflict.\n\n\\paragraph{Tokenizer-agnostic type masking.}\nWe use \\emph{continuation masking} to handle context-dependent tokenizations and prefix-shared types (e.g., \\textsc{Sen}/\\textsc{Sid}/\\textsc{Side} in WikiEvents): a compile-time prefix map identifies valid continuations for each type name, disambiguating over multiple steps across byte-level BPE (Qwen, Llama) and SentencePiece (Mistral).\n\n\\paragraph{Grammar-semantic interaction.}\n\\label{sec:interaction}\nThe final mask is $G(t) \\cap S(t)$; when empty, grammar-first fallback applies (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}), embodying this interaction: semantic constraints reduce DR by only 24\\% without grammar-enforced vocabulary.\n\n\n\\subsection{Decoding Strategies}\n\\label{sec:strategies}\n\n\\paragraph{S1 (joint extraction): Single-pass decoding.}\nBoth layers are active throughout generation; the entity registry populates during entity decoding and is immediately available for relation-level type checking.\n\n\\paragraph{S2 (two-pass pipeline): Two-pass decoding.}\\label{sec:s2}\nA first pass extracts entities; these compile an input-dependent grammar for the second pass.\nS1 achieves higher R-F1 at comparable DR (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}) and is the primary configuration.\n",
